Cardiff City 3 – 3 West Ham | Final whistle, first impressions

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

IT took all of eleven minutes for the new manager bubble to burst as the home side opened the scoring and the Cardiff City fans duly launched into ‘you’re getting sacked in the morning’ to welcome West Ham’s Scott Parker into the dugout. To be fair, a cracking strike from Jack Moylan gave Hermansen no chance and as is now customary, the Hammers found themselves playing catch up.

Parker’s West Ham had set up with a back four holding a surprisingly high line, plus Magassa and Engels playing in front of the defenders but the pair were still bisected by the excellent pass that put Moylan through for his goal.

New manager, same misery as three went in before half time – again

Magassa and Kilman went into the book early on, the young Frenchman in particular looking short of match pace. Taty looked isolated – as we’d feared – and the Hammers looked a little short of ideas the half hour mark, hardly venturing into their opponent’s 18 yard box.

The warning signs were there as the awful Morato was beaten a couple of times as the Bluebirds attacked down the left: The stand in left back duly gave away a penalty, again out of position and clambering all over the back of his winger. Two-nil with Hermansen again beaten from a spot kick.

A third came from a corner, headed in at the near post by Moylan – a hat trick in the first half – and Scott Parker might have been questioning his rush to assume control ahead of the international break: Three nil before half time, three goals conceded for a third week in a row and the Hammers booed off by their own fans again.

Piroe warming up with Kanté and Mayers showed three changes at half time as Magassa, Morato and Taty departed.

Mukasa’s lobbed goal after being put through by Bowen put the Irons back in the game, with still half an hour to go and at least giving the travelling supporters something to cheer. Bowen, playing more centrally, started to pull the strings and Piroe went close. Suddenly, from nothing, a Bowen through pass was flicked on by Piroe and Engles, through on goal slotted home like a veteran striker. 3-2: And thousands of Hammers armchair fans were thinking maybe, just maybe.

This was, at least, a fighting response to a half time dressing down and a well deserved barracking from the away fans.

Kanté almost levelled down one end, Hermansen inexplicably flew out and clattered Kilman down the other – almost giving away a fourth in the process- and suddenly, you couldn’t take your eyes off it.

And then – three – three and the roof came off thanks to Max Kilman’s goal from Bowen’s brilliant cross.

Absolutely chaotic scenes in the away end as the incredulous claret and blue army couldn’t believe they’d witnessed a fightback from a team dead and buried at half time.

It could have been four as Trott stretched his fingers to Soucek’s header – agonisingly close as the minutes ticked by. Brilliant second half efforts by Piroe, by Bowen – the man of the match – and excellent impact from Ezra Mayers who might just have found himself a role in Parker’s side.

Nuno’s team in the first half, Parker’s in the  second.

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

The 4-2-3-1 system leaves the fullbacks exposed. Our 1st half fullbacks aren’t young or pacy and they needed support that the formation didn’t provide. That said they’ve been our best wide defensive players in other systems. We were too narrow and too focused on playing possession football in the 1st half. That might well be how Parker wants the team to play but we aren’t there yet. Good half time adjustments and excellent 2nd half spirit.

What I was particularly pleased about is that although we snatched a draw in spectacular fashion, there were no player celebrations, just long faces, no smiles. They know even this is not good enough. We are West Ham playing Cardiff in a half empty stadium. We should not struggle, we can not be happy with a draw under any circumstances. We need a winning mentality.

Maybe Jack Moylan is seriously good and we had not done our homework on Cardiff.

Lesson given and learnt then we started playing. Now just need to score three in both halves for the rest of our games and Scotty has done a good job.

How is kante not the first name on the team sheet after Bowen and mavropanos? He’s got everything and I really think the boy will have a distinguished career. Shocking first half, abysmal. Second half could have been more of a cricket score than a comeback. Utterly different halves of football. What I would say is austbo has to start and Solomon needs to look at himself. Not the finished article yet but the little Norwegian wants to make things happen. Anyone know if Alvarez is injured or just exiled?

Because he lopes like a giraffe, has poor control, has little anticipation, and he makes rash challenges. He’s not a number 10 and his game doesn’t mesh with Castellanos. On the positive side he has some pace, energy, potential, and unpredictability. He’s much better coming forward from a deeper defensive midfield role next to Engels. He was good tonight. The last 2 games…not so much.

Solomon has had several poor games in a row. He has a higher ceiling than Austbo but he lacks the work-rate and willingness to defend that Bowen demonstrates.

Thank God he took Taty off, he has been coasting for a while now and Piroe showed his real worth, not wasting possession. always looking to play others in and a shot or two.

Mayers, at last playing at LB!! No problems defending, quick & physical enough to help down the left wing – really bright game.

Bowen, his best game of the season, ran the show, brilliant!

Thought Kilman should have come off at half-time – boy, was I wrong.

Fun seeing Noble and Parker after the match, constantly chatting, already planning, assessing, thinking ahead. Going to enjoy the ups and downs of the season knowing we have some wholehearted WHU stalwarts running the show.

Noticeable that our “strikers” failed to score, yet again ! What, exactly, does Castellanos get paid for ? Don’t get carried away by the second half performance. Just remember the first half & realize that we are a poor team, at the moment. You can’t simply give the opposition a big lead & then expect to be able to overcome them in the end. Typical West Ham performance: but WHY ?

Liverpool Have the Next Gerrard and Torres Who Can Save Andoni Iraola £200m

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola could reap the benefits of an exciting duo that some Liverpool fans have compared to Steven Gerrard and Fernando Torres.

The Reds have enjoyed a relatively solid start to life under the Spanish head coach, who replaced Arne Slot in the summer. Having lost only twice in 14 games in all competitions, there are plenty of positives to take.

However, there are a few positions in the squad that either require major surgery or extra quality depth. Two of those are central midfield and up front. But that might not be the case for long.

Iraola showed he's more than willing to give youth a chance during his time at Bournemouth. The Spaniard has continued that trend at Anfield, handing big roles to teenagers Rio Ngumoha and Trey Nyoni in the opening months of the season.

One man who is certainly making a case to be the next in line for significant first-team minutes is Will Wright. The 18-year-old striker joined the Reds from Salford City for an initial £200,000 in 2025, and is now showing an incredible eye for the back of the net.

The Egyptian King has now played 10 games since his Liverpool exit, and plenty has been said about it.

On Wednesday afternoon, Wright fired in a hat-trick against Fenerbahce in the UEFA Youth League, having already notched a treble in the competition. His tally in all competitions for the youth side is seven goals and three assists in 12 appearances.

His third goal against Fenerbahce summed up the impressive Englishman, who latched onto a through ball and fired past the goalkeeper like an experienced striker. Watch it below.

Many Liverpool supporters have taken just as much interest in the player who threaded the ball through to Wright after a powerful run in midfield. Luca Eden, a 15-year-old midfielder, has already drawn comparisons to one of the club's greatest-ever players, Steven Gerrard.

Watch the clip above, some have suggested it took them back to the glory days of Gerrard and Torres, a partnership that excited fans and combined for many goals at Anfield.

Eden is already playing with players above his age group and looking comfortable, with his powerful strides and passing ability reminiscent of Gerrard.

It's extremely early days for the two youngsters, but if they progress to become the players fans expect, then Liverpool have two future stars in their ranks.

In 2026, the going rate for a top-class centre-forward is upwards of £100 million. A reliable goalscorer is worth their weight in gold, especially at the top level. Wright is showing early promise that he can become exactly that.

When Liverpool eventually look to replace Alexander Isak as the starting striker, Hugo Ekitike looks best placed to fill that void. By that time, Wright could be more than ready to play second fiddle to the Frenchman.

Found an error? Send it info@www.givemesport.com so it can be corrected.

Eden's chances of making it on the red side of Merseyside are harder to gauge given his age. However, midfielders like Enzo Fernandez (twice), Declan Rice and Elliot Anderson have all moved for £100 million or more. Living up to Steven Gerrard's name is almost impossible, but getting anywhere near that level would save Iraola and Liverpool from splashing out on another huge transfer fee.

17 better ways Elon Musk could have wasted $446 million

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

One of the basic problems of spending too much time on social media is that you are cooking your brain by reading posts from a pack of attention-seeking lunatics. If your social media of choice is the site formerly known as Twitter, and it is your main source of contact with the outside world, you may delusionally throw away nearly $500 million dollars on midterm elections, just like Elon Musk.

In particular, $47 million of that money from Musk’s America PAC was aimed at defeating Texas Democrat James Talarico, who trounced Musk’s preferred Senate candidate, Ken Paxton, with 55 percent of the vote. Talarico even made Musk’s influence a central part of his campaign ads, with the slogan “Texas is not for sale.”

The opposition to data centers is a way of making concrete how pissed off most people are with the tech industry

How did Musk get this so wrong? I blame the bubble of X. My former colleague Casey Newton, who has (with some well-earned shame) slunk back to X, explained that he did so because it was the site that was most influential with the AI crowd. In some sense, this is exactly the problem. The agentmaxxing loons of AI influencing are wildly out of touch with normal people. There is, right now, one very important bipartisan position in the United States, and it is: Fuck a data center.

It’s pretty obvious that the opposition to data centers is a way of making concrete how pissed off most people are with the tech industry, particularly with the gangster tech regulation of late and the constant stream of threats coming from AI companies. These guys have really tied themselves and their AI aspirations to Donald Trump’s sinking ship, and if you’re hanging out on X all the time, that’s impossible to see, not least because the site’s owner, Musk, is shaping the algorithm in ways that make him feel good.

Since Musk seems to have forgotten what it is like to experience joy, or even mild satisfaction, I have taken the liberty to make a list of things he could have bought to get him a better return on his investment. He probably could flip several things on this list for more money, but at minimum he could have gotten a little rush of pleasure from a new purchase:

But hey, at least changing the name of SpaceX’s “Superintelligence” division back to “Artificial Intelligence” is free!

Musk spent last night deleting tweets so he wouldn’t look as stupid as he is. Too bad it’s not as easy to delete other things! Texans were already asking questions about sweetheart deals to benefit Musk, and now there’s a wave of politicians who ran against his money coming into power, including the governor and the attorney general of Texas. That may mean unredacting Gov. Greg Abbott’s Musk emails, for instance — answering some questions about the influence Musk bought with Texas Republicans.

So actually, maybe the best investment on that list would be the dream team of lawyers who clobbered Musk on the Twitter deal — because Musk has a bunch of new enemies in power, and the simplest thing in the world they can do to curry favor with their voters is investigate him. Bet you’d never know that if you spent all your time on X.

Player Ratings West Ham v Cardiff: Bowen Inspires Incredible Comeback

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

West Ham produced an extraordinary second-half comeback to rescue a 3-3 draw against Cardiff City, having looked completely shell-shocked as they trudged off at half-time three goals behind. Scott Parker’s first game in charge certainly wasn’t short of incident, with the Hammers seemingly heading for another humiliating defeat.

Joel Moylan’s first-half hat-trick had left West Ham in all sorts of trouble, but three substitutions at the interval helped transform the performance. Goals from Divine Mukasa, Arne Engels and Max Kilman completed a remarkable recovery, with the Hammers almost snatching a winner during a frantic finish.

Jarrod Bowen was the inspiration behind the comeback, although the difference between West Ham’s first and second-half performances was astonishing. Having looked completely devoid of confidence during the opening 45 minutes, the players suddenly discovered some urgency, purpose and attacking quality.

It was certainly a dramatic response to the dreadful performance against West Bromwich Albion, although Parker will have seen plenty of problems that still need addressing.

Made a couple of decent saves and produced one absolutely brilliant long throw that almost released Castellanos. Had little chance with Cardiff’s opening goal, but his decision to charge out of his penalty area, miss the ball completely and collide with Kilman was unnecessarily rash. Another unconvincing performance.

Steady and assured defensively in the first half, but his understanding with Bowen was almost nonexistent. The pair looked like they’d never met each other before. Completely different after the break, when Veltman played with real intensity, provided attacking width and combined excellently with the captain. One of West Ham’s most improved performers.

Some excellent defending, including a particularly impressive one-on-one challenge late in the game. His distribution was also a major positive, regularly pinging accurate balls towards the left flank and producing a lovely through ball to create Kanté’s one-on-one opportunity. One of West Ham’s better players across the 90 minutes.

Made an excellent early tackle but was subsequently booked after Solomon’s dreadful loss of possession exposed the defence. May have got a slight deflection on Cardiff’s opening goal, although there was little he could realistically have done. More than made amends in the 81st minute, showing West Ham’s strikers how to finish with a superb half-volley to equalise.

Actually started very well, winning numerous tackles and recovering possession repeatedly. Unfortunately, his lack of pace was badly exposed by a simple ball over the top, leading to a clumsy and avoidable penalty. He’s a centre-back being asked to play left-back, and it showed. Replaced at half-time.

Made his fair share of tackles and demonstrated some of the value of having a specialist defensive midfielder. Produced a crucial last-ditch challenge to prevent Cardiff from potentially scoring again. However, West Ham still left far too much space in central areas and he was withdrawn at half-time.

Worked hard in a box-to-box role but offered surprisingly little of note for much of the match. Played a decent pass in the build-up to one of Kanté’s attacking runs before making a beautifully timed run beyond Cardiff’s defence to score his first West Ham goal. Took the finish very well, and it was a significant contribution.

West Ham’s most technically accomplished player in the first half, looking comfortable in possession and enjoying the freedom of playing as a number ten. Picked out some lovely passes, including an excellent through ball that Castellanos couldn’t capitalise upon. Took his goal brilliantly, controlling Bowen’s chipped pass with his right foot before finishing delicately with his left. A very encouraging performance.

His performance was a microcosm of West Ham’s entire evening. Anonymous and nervous in the first half, but absolutely commanding in the second. His clever chipped pass created Mukasa’s goal, his long ball started the move for Engels’ strike, and his inswinging delivery provided the equaliser for Kilman.

By the closing stages he was practically running the game, producing a wonderful piece of jiggery-pokery to create a late chance for Mayers before delivering another dangerous inswinging ball that Souček headed on target. Outstanding after the break.

Showed some early purpose with a good run down the left, but his decision-making was dreadful. Repeatedly hesitated when presented with opportunities to shoot or pass, and one particularly poor three-yard ball straight to an opponent led to Kilman’s booking. His final deliveries were ineffective and he struggled to make the most of promising positions. Replaced after 67 minutes, and not before time.

Pretty much anonymous throughout the first half. Repeatedly outpaced when through balls offered him opportunities to run beyond Cardiff’s defence, including one occasion when he was beaten for speed by centre-back Will Fish. Managed a tame shot just before the interval but offered very little. Admittedly, West Ham’s disjointed attacking play hardly helped him. Replaced at half-time.

At least attempted to drop deep and get involved, although his first couple of contributions involved giving the ball away. Headed a decent Veltman cross straight at Trott and failed to connect properly with another Bowen delivery. Improved considerably, producing a good shot from distance before supplying an excellent flick-on for Engels’ goal.

Brought tremendous energy and directness after coming on at half-time. Produced an excellent driving run almost half the length of the pitch, followed by some lovely footwork to twist and turn through Cardiff’s defence. Gave the ball away cheaply on occasion but also forced Trott into a very good save after a wonderful first touch created a one-on-one opportunity. Probably did enough to force his way into Parker’s thoughts for the next game.

Introduced at half-time to replace Morato and provide some much-needed pace at full-back. Won an early tackle and looked more comfortable dealing with the demands of the position. Almost scored a dramatic late winner after making an excellent run to meet Bowen’s chipped delivery at the far post, attempting a left-footed instep volley that went tantalisingly close.

Made more of an attacking impression in his first 30 seconds than Solomon had managed for much of the evening. Immediately dropped his shoulder, cut inside and tried his luck with a shot that went wide. In the 89th minute, produced a stinging effort that Trott could only parry back into his penalty area. Confident, positive and willing to take responsibility.

Introduced in the 88th minute for Veltman. Almost scored a dramatic stoppage-time winner when he met another dangerous inswinging Bowen delivery with a well-directed header, forcing Nathan Trott into an excellent save. Not on the pitch long enough to warrant a numerical rating.

I always thought that he was limited as a striker, no pace, strength, great in the air or vicious snapshots on the opposition goal, if he was any of those things, he would be giving the opposition defence something to think about. What Is Rhabdo? What Every Woman Should Know About the Rare Condition

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A woman’s death during the New York City Marathon has put the condition back in the spotlight.

At the New York City Marathon on Sunday, 24-year-old Rachel Apa of Lewisberry, Pennsylvania, collapsed two miles from the finish line. She later died at the hospital.

The medical examiner’s office is still investigating her cause of death. Her father, Steve Apa, told the New York Times that the cause was rhabdomyolysis, commonly known as rhabdo. The rare condition occurs when skeletal muscle breaks down, releasing its contents into the bloodstream, says Robert Glatter, MD, an emergency room doctor and an assistant professor of emergency medicine at Lenox Hill Hospital.

Apa’s death comes amid growing conversations about rhabdo on social media. People have shared their experiences with the condition after intense exercise, particularly at cycling classes.

But while these stories are alarming, rhabdo is still relatively rare. Yet they raise questions about the risks of intense exercise, how to recognize when something is wrong, and just how concerned the everyday exerciser should be. Here’s what experts want you to know.

Rhabdomyolysis happens when skeletal muscle breaks down and releases its contents into the bloodstream. The resulting spill can produce dangerous electrolyte imbalances and kidney injury, particularly when someone is dehydrated, Dr. Glatter says.

Although rhabdo is often associated with intense exercise, physical exertion is just one possible cause. “Rhabdomyolysis can also follow crush injuries, seizures, prolonged immobilization, heat stroke, severe infections, certain drugs or toxins, metabolic abnormalities and, more rarely, inherited muscle disorders,” Dr. Glatter says.

Exercise-related rhabdo typically happens when an individual suddenly performs an unusually difficult, repetitive workout involving large muscle groups—particularly when doing eccentric exercise, or when the muscle contracts while lengthening. The risk may be higher when the activity is unfamiliar or far more intense than the person is accustomed to. Some examples include high-repetition squats, lunges, downhill running, and intense cycling, Dr. Glatter says.

“The key issue is often not simply that the workout was ‘hard,’ but that its intensity or volume was far beyond what that person’s muscles were conditioned to tolerate,” he says.

Symptoms include muscle pain "dramatically out of proportion to ordinary post-workout ache,” Dr. Glatter describes, as well as dark-colored urine, swelling, weakness, and difficulty moving the affected muscles. Other possible symptoms include dizziness, fainting, confusion, persistent vomiting or feeling generally ill, he says. “Someone who develops severe or rapidly worsening muscle pain or swelling after a strenuous workout—especially together with marked weakness, dark urine or substantially decreased urination—should be evaluated urgently.”

It’s important to note that symptoms can start while someone is still exercising. An athlete may notice muscle pain that feels disproportionate to effort, unusual heaviness or weakness, severe fatigue, or a drop in pace or intensity that would normally be manageable, Dr. Glatter says. In severe cases, athletes can collapse, appear confused, or develop nausea, dizziness, and swelling.

Other risk factors also include dehydration, exercising in extreme heat or humidity, a recent viral illness, certain medications or drugs, and underlying genetic or metabolic disorders, such as sickle cell.

Although many recent social media accounts of exercise-related rhabdo have come from women, it’s important to know that an unaccustomed high-intensity workout can overwhelm muscles regardless of sex. Those stories don’t necessarily reflect who is most likely to develop the condition, and studies have shown generally higher rates in men.

It’s complicated. “It’s probably a combination of greater awareness and a genuine increase in some types of exertional rhabdomyolysis, but the evidence does not support describing rhabdo as a new epidemic,” Dr. Glatter says.

Rhabdo has been around for decades, and it’s difficult to determine overall incidence rates because mild cases may never be diagnosed. Still, some research suggests that exercise-related cases have increased in certain settings. A 2022 analysis published in the Physician and Sportsmedicine looked at emergency department data from 2000 through 2019. It estimated more than 40,000 cases of exertional rhabdomyolysis and found an increase over that period.

Dr. Glatter suggests this might be, in part, due to shifts in exercise culture. An increase in the availability of fitness classes such as high-intensity interval training, cycling, and CrossFit-style workouts can expose people to unfamiliar movement patterns at high intensities.

“These workouts are not inherently dangerous, and millions of people perform them safely. The risk arises when workload increases too quickly relative to conditioning, particularly when heat, dehydration, illness, medications or inadequate recovery are added to the equation,” Dr. Glatter says. “A highly motivated newcomer trying to keep pace with a class may continue well past the point at which her muscles are accustomed to working.”

It’s also important to note: Being well-trained does lower the risk of rhabdo, but it does not eliminate it. “In a trained athlete, rhabdomyolysis is often best understood as a ‘multiple-hit’ phenomenon, where several physiologic stresses occur at the same time,” Dr. Glatter says. Someone may be proportionally prepared for the activity, but factors such as unusually high intensity or duration, heat and humidity, dehydration, recent illness, inadequate recovery, electrolyte abnormalities, and certain medications or supplements can lower the threshold for injury. Rare underlying conditions, such as sickle cell trait or certain metabolic conditions, can also predispose a healthy athlete to rhabdo.

That doesn’t mean you should fear strenuous exercise. Starting at a manageable level, building up gradually, and staying hydrated can help reduce the risk.

“The body adapts extraordinarily well to exercise when intensity and volume are increased gradually,” Dr. Glatter says. “Going from relatively little training to an all-out, high-repetition workout involving large muscle groups is a very different physiological challenge.”

Exercise-related rhabdo is uncommon, but can happen when a workout places demands on muscles that are far beyond what they’re accustomed to. High-intensity exercise can be part of a healthy routine; the key is to build toward it gradually and pay attention to how your body responds. If you develop severe muscle pain or swelling, weakness, dark urine, or substantially decreased urination, seek urgent medical care.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

Abdul El-Sayed  Embraced His Swiftie Status All the Way to the U.S. Senate

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Abdul El-Sayed won the heated race for Michigan’s open Senate seat on Tuesday. Some people think his Swiftie status didn’t hurt as he made his way to victory.

This summer, El-Sayed posted one of his most-viewed TikTok videos. In the warm-hued clip, the U.S. Senate hopeful from Michigan danced on a balcony wearing an American-flag printed cowboy hat, jeans, and his trademark tight black V-neck shirt. But it was the song choice that boosted the clip: El-Sayed and his team opted for Taylor Swift‘s “You Belong With Me (Taylor’s Version).” The El-Sayed campaign team changed the lyrics of the song to reflect jabs at his opponent, Mike Rogers, and captioned the video to read: “Happy Taylor wedding …err, July 4th weekend” in a nod to Swift’s big day. It shouldn’t come as a surprise that the video racked up 5.7 million views.

Underneath the clip, several TikTok users claiming to be Swift’s fans left comments like, “Michigan Swifties unite!!!! Proudly voting for you sir 🫶☺️❤️,” “LMAO NOT MILLENNIAL SWIFTIE IN MY POLITICS SIGN ME UP,” and “This swiftie will be voting for you. 🫶” El-Sayed clinched the Democratic nomination for the Senate race in August, just a month after posting the clip. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

That Hole in Your Ruler Isn’t Random – Here’s What It’s Actually For

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

That small hole on a ruler has a practical purpose and an unexpected history. Here's why it exists and how people use it.

Almost everyone has used a ruler at some point, whether it was for a school project, measuring something around the house, or drawing a straight line. But even after years of using one, there’s a small detail that’s easy to miss: the hole near the end.

Those holes in rulers aren’t just part of the design. They actually serve a purpose, and the reason they’re there goes back further than most people might expect. Here’s what they’re for, how they were used in the past, and a few other ways they come in handy.

The hole at the end of a ruler is mainly there for storage. It lets people hang the ruler on a hook, nail, or pegboard instead of leaving it in a drawer or on a workbench. This is particularly helpful with longer metal rulers that might not fit in a toolbox.

But the hole has been around for much longer than modern workshops. Years ago, before paper notebooks became common in schools, students practiced writing on small slate boards. They could wipe away their writing with a damp cloth and use the board again.

Some students tied that cloth to their rulers with a piece of string through the hole. That way, they always had something nearby to clean their slates. Schools eventually moved away from slate boards, but the holes remained. Today, they’re mostly there to make rulers easier to hang and store.

Hanging a ruler isn’t the only reason manufacturers add holes. Some rulers have several, and they can serve different purposes depending on where they’re placed. For example, certain school rulers have three holes spaced along one side. These line up with the rings in a standard three-ring binder, so students can keep their rulers with their other school supplies.

Some rulers also have holes that help with drawing circles. A pin through one hole can hold the ruler in place while a pencil in another hole traces a circle as the ruler turns. Not every ruler has holes designed for this, but specialty models like the Creative Grids Quick Trim Ruler do. A compass is still a better choice when exact measurements matter.

Some crafting rulers have holes for more specific jobs. Quilting rulers and templates may have openings that help mark circles or line up fabric before cutting. Others have slots or shaped edges that guide a rotary cutter, making it easier to cut certain shapes.

There are also special rulers for paper crafts and stitching. Some have rows of tiny, evenly spaced holes that help people mark where to make stitches or decorative patterns.

Of course, not every ruler has holes. Smaller plastic rulers often don’t need them, while longer metal versions may have one at each end. It mostly comes down to how the manufacturer expects people to use the ruler. And for everyday measuring, the holes don’t make much difference. A ruler without them can measure just as accurately as one with them.

VMware releases vSphere Express for virtualization on up to 128 cores

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

“We started listening to a lot of our lower-end customers,” VMware exec says.

After alienating many long-time small- to medium-sized business (SMB) customers, Broadcom’s VMware is trying to appeal to its smaller customers.

Today, the virtualization company announced vSphere Express, a lower-end virtualization offering for organizations that require “cost-effective essential virtualization.”

vSphere Express comes after many customers reported that VMware was pushing them to VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF), the flagship private cloud bundle that has many features SMBs don’t need and is expensive. Some SMB users have reported that VMware sales representatives previously told them that vSphere Standard, VMware’s prior entry-level virtualization offering, wasn’t available anymore.

A VMware spokesperson told The Register that VMware will sell a per-core subscription license at a price that will be “significantly lower” than the VMware vSphere Foundation (VVF) bundle and VCF but wouldn’t provide a price quote.

IT distributor TD Synnex lists vSphere Express as costing $9,600 a year for running on up to 128 cores. The price doesn’t get cheaper with fewer cores, which still presents a challenge to the smallest use cases.

vSphere Express includes basic capabilities, like vMotion, for “live migration of running virtual machines with improved migration speed and efficiency,” and high availability for the “automated restart of failed virtual machines,” VMware’s announcement said.

The product only supports discrete instances, and organizations won’t be able to manage them centrally, Prashanth Shenoy, VP of product marketing for VCF, said, according to The Register.

“We started listening to a lot of our lower-end customers who didn’t really want the VVF but wanted the core virtualization capability, and [vSphere Express is] really meant for that market,” Shenoy said, per SDxCentral.

Whereas vSphere Standard hasn’t seen a meaningful update in years, vSphere Express will be updated as frequently as VCF and VVF are, The Register reported.

Returning focus to a streamlined low-end virtualization product is an about-face for VMware, which has largely focused on enterprise-size customers since Broadcom’s takeover. This focus shift has been evident as the tech giant has reduced the number of channel partners that it works with, bundled its product lineup into a small number of expensive SKUs, and canceled plans to take its biggest clients direct.

While VMware has been financially successful under Broadcom, vSphere Express suggests Broadcom may worry about losing business from smaller organizations and, more broadly, that its changes are pushing customers of various sizes to explore their options.

In September, while teasing what would become vSphere Express, Paul Turner, VCF’s chief product officer, told The Register that Broadcom issued sales incentives that pushed its sales team to sell VCF. He said that VMware had “too big a focus on VCF.”

Nick Watson, chief technologist at UK-based tech advisory firm C4C Group, told TechTarget today: “My reading is that Express exists because customers looked at VCF, did the sums, and started packing. This is Broadcom putting a door back on the building.”

Migrating off VMware can be very costly and time-consuming, making matters particularly complicated for SMBs. For organizations that can’t currently juggle a migration alongside other projects and/or have limited resources, vSphere Express can be a much simpler and more easily attainable solution than changing virtualization platforms.

Some SMBs, though, including the thousands of firms that have already migrated to VMware rivals and firms whose preferred resellers no longer work with VMware, will be much harder to sway, though.

“Broadcom has lost all credibility. No thanks. We have two more locations to migrate off VMware and we’re done with this nightmare. Not playing these games,” a Reddit user said last week in response to details shared about vSphere Express.

Speaking with Ars Technica in September, Dean Colpitts, CTO of Canadian managed services provider and former VMware partner Members IT Group, noted that even though migrating to the new low-end virtualization offering may be easy, Broadcom has “already shown both customers and partners they can’t be trusted.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

GTA 6 Has Already Sold Nearly 8 Million Copies, One Data Firm Claims

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Grand Theft Auto 6Grand Theft Auto VIGTA 6PS5Rockstar GamesSalesXbox Series X/S

We are two weeks out from the start of Grand Theft Auto 6‘s global launch, and one data firm is claiming it’s already sold nearly 8 million copies. Not surprisingly, it also claims roughly three quarters of those sales are on PlayStation 5.

Data firm Sensor Tower has updated its GTA 6 sales tracker and currently has Rockstar Games’ latest open-world crime simulator at an estimated 7.71 million copies sold via pre-orders alone. That comes out to an estimated $751 million in revenue, with Sensor Tower claiming a breakdown of roughly 74 percent of copies sold on PS5 compared to 26 percent on Xbox Series X/S.

The trendline shows an uptick in the rate of pre-orders sold following the extended gameplay reveal on Netflix, and it sure seems like Sony’s exclusive marketing campaign for GTA 6 has helped give it a bump over Xbox. The fact that a base PS5 is cheaper than a base Xbox Series X could also be a factor. According to Sensor Tower, 1.97 million copies have been pre-ordered on Xbox compared to 5.74 million on PS5.

Given Xbox’s struggles this console generation, that number isn’t great, but it could be much worse. Especially when it has to resort to sticking hardware inside of an alligator to get around marketing prohibitions on talking about GTA 6 coming to Xbox Series X/S.

To put the overall estimate of 7.71 million pre-order sales in perspective, Cyberpunk 2077 reportedly amassed around 8 million total pre-orders in the run up to its 2020 launch. That number also already puts it just behind Resident Evil Requiem‘s estimated 8.5 million sales which, for now at least, are enough to make Capcom’s horror sequel the second best-selling game of 2026 in the U.S. so far.

And it’s just a small chunk of what some analysts are predicting for total launch window sales. Financial services firm Piper Sandler is predicting 45 million sales when GTA 6 comes out. Anything less might be seen as disappointing, even if it would still be well over the $800 million GTA 5 made during its first 24 hours. It’s going to be the biggest game launch ever. We’ll see if it ends up being big enough for Take-Two’s investors.

This California Tuning Shop Built an Insane Lexus GX–Toyota Tacoma Off-Road Pickup Truck

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The “Lexus TX1” is equal parts GX 550, Taco, and trophy truck.

The Toyota Tacoma is one of the most popular mid-size pickup trucks on sale in America today, with a wide range of variants. But what if there were a luxury version? What if it were built by Lexus? California tuning shop Meso Customs has brought an answer to that line of questioning with its 2026 SEMA Show build, and the results are surprisingly compelling. It’s called the Lexus TX1, and it is immediately recognizable as the forbidden love child of a Lexus GX 550 and a fourth-generation Toyota Tacoma.

As Toyota’s luxury wing, Lexus does not have a pickup in its lineup; premium trucks tend to sell best when they have non-luxury badges. That said, the TX1 features horizontal grille slats and a prominent silver Lexus badge that somehow all seem to blend in and work together well. On top of all that, a rally-style light bar on top of the windshield and a high-clearance metal front bumper show the TX1 means business.

The project started as a CGI rendering, according before evolving into a full build a year later, with Meso Customs using CAD designs and fabricated individual pieces to make the TX1 a reality. But this isn’t a simple body kit build, either. The Yuba City, California-based shop added a lift kit from King Off-Road Racing Shocks, 74Weld portal axles, and 38-inch Toyo Tires.

The rear end is equally stunning, with a straight-across LED light bar from the GX550 and a tucked bumper. The bed was actually custom-built to incorporate the shape of a GX hatch into the Tacoma shell, while Baja Designs lights were integrated underneath and to the side of the wing mirrors. Meso Customs even displayed the TX1 with a color-matched Honda Grom in the bed, to show just how practical a Lexus pickup truck could be.

Of course, the TX1 build was not officially sponsored by Toyota or Lexus, so don’t expect a luxury-style Tacoma derivative from the Japanese automaker anytime soon. Even so, the idea that Lexus could build a proper off-road pickup with a luxurious interior is one fun fantasy. And if you’re inclined to see it in person, Meso Customs currently has the Lexus TX1 on display at the Toyo Tires Treadpass exhibit at the 2026 SEMA Show.

A New York transplant hailing from the Pacific Northwest, Emmet White has a passion for anything that goes: cars, bicycles, planes, and motorcycles. After learning to ride at 17, Emmet worked in the motorcycle industry before joining Autoweek in 2022 and Road & Track in 2024. The woes of alternate side parking have kept his fleet moderate, with a 2014 Volkswagen Jetta GLI and a BMW 318i E30 street parked in his Queens community.

What I Didn’t Tell You About My Flip-Flop

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

I left off writing at the edge of the 100-Mile Wilderness. I planned two more posts, one about the Hundred-Mile, the last about Katahdin, but I couldn’t write them.

After Katahdin I was home, as I’d planned to be. I’d be back in Wisconsin a week, get another cortisone shot for my arthritic knee, spend time with my wife, see friends, then catch Amtrak back to Pawling and push south to Springer.

That was the plan, the one I told you about. But the truth is, by the time I hit Maine, I’d stopped believing it.

I had my reasons, and they were good ones.

Heading south would have meant finishing in December and a flip within my flop to get through the Smokies before snow turned the trail into a post-holing nightmare.

Then there were my spring classes to prep (I still teach), my wife’s birthday, an election to vote in, and one more Boundary Waters trip to take. Plus, I looked forward to resuming my barista job. I was excited about improving my latte art and counter skills.

Better to come back next year, I told myself. Better weather, more time, and a straight shot to Georgia. I would finish the first week of November. Perfect.

I’d thru-hiked the PCT and the CDT. I was fine doing the AT as a two-part LASH. I had nothing to prove.

Unlike Killington in 2024, getting off the AT this time wasn’t a surrender. It was a conscious choice. I would be back next summer, but I would return as a section hiker. And to my surprise, that mattered. A lot.

Many of us deal with depression after getting off trail. I am a member of this sad clan. On trail I made new friends, shared parts of myself that hadn’t seen the light of day in years, shed pounds, and became crazy-fit while eating like a joyful pig.

Being on trail expanded my life and gave it purpose by shrinking it. The daily routine of miles and chores, the resupply stops of splurge and cleansing, and the slow accretion of accomplishment.

But now, back home and staying, that bigger life has abruptly ended. The better version of myself, the one who crushed miles despite the bad joints, is slowly fading.

I’ve been following Guru on Instagram, the guy in the kilt I hiked the 100-Mile Wilderness and summited Katahdin with. Seeing his photos of autumn sunsets, fall-stripped trees and leaf-strewn trails, I can smell the damp leaves, feel the cold air. I think, “That could have been me.”

Getting through the Whites showed me what my old body could do. My confidence only grew as I clambered through the Mahoosucs, the Bigelows, and hard sections with no name. I flew over the roots and rocks of Maine, nearly ecstatic. When I climbed Katahdin, I was at my peak.

There are no guarantees I’ll come back and finish. Not for lack of desire, but because of my waning ability. My arthritis, my stamina, and who knows what other shoe might drop between now and next July.

I now believe I should have kept going. It’s not just that I can’t trust my body; it’s like I broke a promise to myself.

When I set out to hike the Triple Crown in 2022, the subtext read “thru-hike.” My dad’s passing, and my AT attempt in 2024 that followed, changed the rules. It seemed that just finishing, no matter the method, was all that mattered.

I am a thru-hiker. An identity of which I am ridiculously proud. I’ve had my face wind-blasted raw below whooshing turbines outside Tehachapi, been chased by wildfires through Oregon, and dragged a norovirus-weakened body through the Cascades. On the CDT, I pushed through joint pain requiring oral steroids in the Wind River Range and dunked frost-stiffened shoes into the Gila River so I could jam my frozen feet into them. To get off trail 6,000 miles into my Triple Crown, even by choice, now feels like a betrayal of what I set out to do.

It took me a long time to write this post. I’d planned to keep writing my way to Katahdin, a story I still need to tell. But I had to come clean first. Thank you for following me this far.

Next time, I’ll tell you about the Hundred-Mile.

Todd “Doctor Za” Wellnitz is a long-distance hiker and writer pursuing the Triple Crown in his 60s, with thru-hikes of the PCT and CDT behind him, and the AT still a work in progress. Two joint replacements later, he's still moving. His writing explores how time on trail reshapes our sense of self, our assumptions about effort and pain, and how we make decisions under uncertainty.

Norway Gave Us Skiing—and the Gear to Match

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Here’s something to share during après chatter: The word “ski” comes from Norwegian, and archaeologists trace the prehistoric origins of the sport to the Sami people, who used skis for hunting and travel across what would become Scandinavia. Later, the Norwegian military turned the practice into a sport, complete with races. No wonder Norway dominates so many ski disciplines at the winter Olympics.

It’s also no wonder that Helly Hansen, the legendary weather-protection gear maker, extended their apparel line to ski gear after developing waterproof oilskins almost 150 years ago. With a relentless focus on developing new materials and designs that improve comfort and performance, Helly Hansen’s latest products—like these four pieces—are the season’s secret weapon for getting the most fun out of the snow.

Inventing skiing and the best winter gear should be enough. But to really seal its legacy as the champion of the sport, Helly Hansen also wants you to Ski Free. With the purchase of an eligible pair of pants or jacket, get a complimentary one-day ski pass at one of more than 80 participating ski resorts worldwide.

One of the best jackets for resort skiing gets even better this year. The Men’s Alpha/Women’s Alphelia is lighter, warmer, and even more breathable, thanks to upgraded Lifaloft synthetic insulation that packs down tighter without sacrificing water resistance or performance. Just-right details (detachable helmet-compatible hood and powder skirt, ski pass pocket, underarm vents) now include high-tech safety features like the Life Pocket+, which helps keep your phone alive in the cold, and a RECCO Advanced Rescue System to help others find you in an emergency.

When it comes to gear, you have to earn the name “Legendary”—and these best-selling pants do, with features and flex that adapt to unpredictable conditions. Two-way stretch helps you dig into deep turns without feeling restricted, and the Helly Tech Performance shell fabric locks out precip while shedding moisture from the inside. Primaloft insulation keeps you plenty warm on long lift rides, but you can also dump heat: Lengthy thigh zippers offer venting for strenuous uphill climbs or leg-burning descents on warmer days.

Everyone needs a versatile midlayer. Make yours this one—it transitions perfectly from shoulder-season hiking to winter skiing. In place of conventional pile-and-pill fleece, the unique grid-pattern fleece traps heat and breathes well, keeping you in the comfort zone through changes in temperature and effort without having to drop or swap a layer. A handy chest pocket and slim, stylish profile round out the all-purpose design.

Ever heard a ski patroller complain about cold fingers? Of course not—they know they need gloves that work for both warmth and dexterity, which is why Helly Hansen teamed up with top ski patrol teams to design a glove that does the job all day, even in horrendous conditions. Fully waterproof, breathable, and insulated with Primaloft Black Eco, the Odin glove has reinforced overlays in high-wear areas like the palm and forefingers to increase grip and durability. Fleece lining ensures an oh-so-soft fit, and included carabiner and finger loops keep these gloves out of the snow and on your belt when off.

Founded in Norway in 1877, Helly Hansen has been making professional grade gear to help people stay and feel alive in the harshest environments around the world. Helly Hansen is a leader in performance ski apparel and technical sailing, as well as premium outdoor gear.

Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: New features and other upgrades to expect

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Linux

It starts with a surprise version number: Linux Mint 27 instead of 24. But the next release brings far more changes than that.

Linux Mint, my favorite Linux desktop distribution, is ready for a fresh start.

Its lead developer, Clement “Clem” Lefebvre, announced earlier this year that the team behind it was considering slowing Mint’s release cadence because the old twice-a-year release cycle was time-consuming and constrained their ability to make major changes. So it is that the next release, Linux Mint 27, will appear in your Christmas stocking.

Also: 5 premium Linux distros absolutely worth paying for in 2026

The developers officially changed the designation on Oct. 13th when Mint changed both its release cadence and its numbering. Clem’s happy about the change: it “was a tough decision to make, but we’re really starting to see it pay off. We’re taking on projects and implementing changes which would previously have been considered too large or ambitious.”

The new Mint will use Ubuntu 26.04 Long Term Support (LTS) as its base. As before, it will offer Cinnamon, Xfce, and MATE desktops.

Earlier development snapshots had been labeled Mint 23 “Alfa.” That title was explicitly a placeholder while the team decided its release strategy. Its final name is Adrien. Moving ahead, release numbers will be based on the upcoming year. Clem said, “We’ll no longer use point releases, so the release after that will be 28. You’ll upgrade from 27 to 28 to 29, and so on.” Instead of girls’ names, Mint will use boys’ names for distro titles.

On Halloween, Clem announced the top changes in Mint 27. It starts with Mint bringing hibernation back into its supported desktop configuration through a dedicated Sleep Modes page in its System Administration tool. The page is intended to make enabling and configuring hibernation easier, including addressing its swap-space requirements.

It may not sound like much, but Clem explained it’s actually rather a big deal. “When you close the lid of your laptop, it goes into a sleep mode called “Suspend”. This mode is very useful… but it has a major limitation: It uses power.” People often forget it’s not really off and that your laptop will eventually run out of power. When that happens, “the outcome is worse than a normal shutdown. The session is gone, and the battery is empty.”

Also: Linux Mint vs. RefreshOS: I compared the two best distros for new users

That’s where “Hibernation comes into play. It saves the session to disk and completely shuts down the computer. No power is used. When you reopen the lid, the session is as it was, and so is the battery.”

Another new feature is Superlight. This fresh system-monitoring application replaces the older GNOME System Monitor. It comes with CPU, RAM, swap, GPU, network, disk I/O, temperature, and fan-speed graphs. It also includes per-application and per-process resource views. Battery-equipped systems also get charge and power-flow history.

Its storage analysis is particularly interesting: it can break down disk usage into files and folders, Steam games, and Flatpak applications. That brings several common troubleshooting tasks — “What’s using the CPU?” and “Where did my disk space go?” — into one interface. I’ve been checking it out on a Mint virtual machine (VM), and I like it already.

The Nemo file manager is getting a media preview pane, toggled with F7 or a status-bar button. It supports pictures, audio, and video, and displays associated information — for photographs, that can include the capture date and camera model. I like this feature a lot. I’ve always done a lot of work from file managers going all the way back to the classic Norton Commander and its Linux successor, Midnight Commander.

I’ve said it before, I’ll say it again. You don’t need to ever touch a Linux shell via a terminal to use Mint. But if, like me, you’re an old-school Linux user and you like using the terminal, Velocitty, Mint’s new terminal application, looks interesting. Its announced features include:

The point is to make recurring projects and SSH sessions easier to find and reopen. If you like other terminal programs, my latest fave is the Rust-based WexTerm; you don’t have to use Velocitty.

Unfold is a new archive manager for viewing, creating, and extracting archives. It uses 7-Zip and 7zip-RAR and supports the encryption formats those backends handle, including password-protected archives. Its design goal is simplicity rather than a sprawling feature set.

The new Mint uses Ubuntu 26.04 LTS as its foundation. Mint is also moving from Ubiquity to the Linux Mint Debian Edition live installer for installing the operating system. This fresh installer comes with support for OEM bulk-produced installations, installation on both BIOS and EFI PCs, support for Secure Boot, and LVM/LUKS configurations.

Also: Linux Mint vs. Zorin OS: I’ve tried both Windows alternatives, and here’s my winner

For the Linux kernel, Mint 27 will use version 7.0. Which one exactly? Good question. We’ll see closer to Christmas.

Wayland compatibility remains the goal, but X11 isn’t disappearing. As Clem said in June, “In an ideal scenario, we manage to make Wayland work just as well as X11.” But they’re not there yet.

The central change is a cleanup of existing code. Mint’s longer cycle gives developers room to overhaul several parts of the desktop together, rather than squeezing individual improvements into six-month windows. As Clem put it in the September newsletter, “I don’t think I’ve ever seen so many changes and new features implemented for a single release.”

What that means for you and me will be better laptop power management, more capable built-in utilities, and a more complete collection of everyday desktop applications. For example, I expect the updated Mint will ship with GIMP 3.2.2 as its default photo editor, instead of Mint 22.3’s years-old GIMP 2.10.

andrés jaque questions the politics of utopia in an age of permanent connectivity

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

from archigram’s technological utopias to today’s infrastructures of control, OFFPOLINN's founder explores how architecture can challenge the systems shaping our collective and environmental realities.

Utopias have a tendency to become less interesting as they become real.  At first, they are exhilarating. They overturn conventions, ridicule authorities, and embarrass the present. Their authors anticipate what everyone else has not yet learned to desire. But sometimes, they happen. Archigram. Its visions of technological freedom, mobile architectures, continuous connectivity, and cities emancipated from fixed institutions were among the twentieth century’s most energizing propositions. Their irreverence was inseparable from their architects’ intelligence and biological vitality. Young minds were having enormous fun imagining the demolition of established orders, their hearts racing at possible worlds.

Now consider permanent connectivity. Work finds us everywhere. Platforms promise choice while concentrating power. Technologies celebrate freedom while organizing surveillance, extraction, segregation, and uninterrupted availability. The promise of mobility encounters selective and segregation-making fortified borders. The promise of flexibility encounters a housing crisis that displaces millions. Sophisticated computation accompanies warfare and the destruction of cities. Networks promising universal knowledge also industrialize disinformation.

What once looked like liberation can become an infrastructure of domination. Utopia did not simply fail. Its realization changed the politics of its promises. A utopia does not carry its politics intact into the future.

Blaming Archigram would be absurd. But the resemblance is disturbing. What once looked like liberation can become an infrastructure of domination. Utopia did not simply fail. Its realization changed the politics of its promises. A utopia does not carry its politics intact into the future. Something else has changed. Today’s crises emerge through interactions among computational systems, biological processes, economic institutions, and planetary ecologies. Housing is shaped by financial algorithms, property regimes, migration, and bodies struggling to survive. Climate instability connects atmospheric processes, energy infrastructures, industrial production, governance and predictive computation. Biodiversity loss involves agriculture, logistics, microbial ecologies, and land finance. Warfare combines automated detection with the destruction of bodies, habitats, and infrastructures.

These are not separate technological, social, and environmental problems. They are posthuman enactments in which computation and biology, sensing and decision-making, are inseparable. We struggle to imagine our participation in transformations whose production exceeds what we can sense. Yet we participate constantly. This makes experimentation at the thresholds of sensing an architectural question.

Experimenting now means entering the conflicts through which reality is being produced, rather than imagining alternatives from a safe distance. It means challenging what computational systems recognize, what institutions exclude, what bodies endure, and what other forms of life make possible. It means transforming not only what we know, but who and what can produce knowledge, intervene in decisions, and redirect their consequences. The radicality of architecture lies in making reality itself a site of collective experimentation, where the power to sense, contest, and transform is not merely imagined or proposed but enacted and bodyenvironmentally lived.

We struggle to imagine our participation in transformations whose production exceeds what we can sense. Yet we participate constantly.

Andrés Jaque is a Spanish architect, researcher, and educator, and the founder of the Office for Political Innovation (OFFPOLINN), an international architecture practice established in 2003 and based in New York and Madrid. His work approaches architecture as a political and environmental practice, examining the complex relationships between people, technology, ecology, and the built environment. Moving between architecture, research, performance, and exhibition-making, Jaque explores how design can make visible the social and environmental systems that shape everyday life. He is Professor and Dean of Columbia University’s Graduate School of Architecture, Planning and Preservation. His distinctions include the 2016 Frederick Kiesler Prize for Architecture and the Arts and the Silver Lion for Best Research Project at the 14th Venice Architecture Biennale.

This article is part of designboom’s POSTHUMAN UTOPIAS chapter, exploring what happens when humans are no longer the measure of all things. These stories rethink our relationship with the planet through interspecies architecture, living materials, regenerative landscapes and emerging technologies – learn more here! The chapter is presented in partnership with INDX|GLOBAL – a global cultural platform placing Indian architecture, design, and creative intelligence in conversation with the world.

Each designboom editorial series explores one theme in depth through projects, interviews, features and news across architecture, design, art and technology. Discover more chapters here, and get in touch if you’d like to submit a story through our submissions portal or contact form.

Star Trek Cast A Real-Life American Hero In The Perfect Role

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In the image above, you'll see real-life Georgian politician Stacey Abrams, the minority leader of the state's House of Representatives from 2011 through 2017. She played the president of a united Earth in a notable 2022 episode of "Star Trek: Discovery."

The final episode of the fourth season of "Discovery" was called "Coming Home," and it wrapped up a story arc involving the mysterious Species 10-C, an alien species so cognitively and physiologically different from average humanoids that communication proved near-impossible. The species was so different, in fact, it took months of research to get them right.

Species 10-C was responsible for unleashing a widespread gravitational cataclysm called the Dark Matter Anomaly, which was unwittingly poised to do untold destruction on Earth and other worlds. A lot of diplomacy and panic were involved in trying to contact Species 10-C and explain to them what they were doing. /Film reviewed the season, and found it to be a tricky balance between the cosmic and the personal.

Throughout the season, there is chatter about evacuating Earth, and mention of the planet's president. In the 32nd century, when "Discovery" takes place, Earth will still be united, and will have a singular presidential figure overseeing its government. The president appears at the end of "Coming Home" to put a button on the whole season, reiterating that communication and diplomacy are very, very good indeed. It's wholly fitting that a real-life politician like Stacey Abrams should be willing to appear as that character.

Abrams famously ran for governor of Georgia in 2018, but narrowly lost to Republican Brian Kemp. She ran again in 2022, but lost to Kemp a second time. She has long been an outspoken advocate for voting rights, and founded the organization Fair Fight Action, which aims to fight against voter suppression. She's quite well-suited to appear in the futuristic utopia of "Star Trek."

The end of "Coming Home" involves Earth rejoining the United Federation of Planets, an organization that had fallen by the wayside in the previous few centuries. Stacey Abrams has a scene with Captain Michael Burnham (Sonequa Martin-Green), the show's lead character in which the President of United Earth is presented as somewhat regal, although Abrams, not an actress, brings a grounded officiousness to the role as well. When she says, "United Earth is ready right now to rejoin the Federation, and nothing could make me happier than to say those words," it sounds like a speech that a 21st-century Earth politician might say.

Martin-Green was blown away to be working with Abrams, something she gushed about in an interview with Deadline. She said:

"I'm still floored when I think about Stacey gracing us with her presence in our Season 4 finale. [...] We spent so much of our time fanning out and pretending to be cool in her midst. [...] She's a legend in the making and a civil hero, and she was an absolute delight to work with. [...] She wowed us with her charm, humility, and generosity, and she whipped out some acting chops too! [...] It was an honor for me as a Black woman to stand with her in the story. It's an experience I'll cherish for the rest of my life."

Abrams is cherished throughout the American political landscape. In 2019, she was selected to deliver the response to the President's State of the Union address, and she was even buzzed about as a potential running mate for presidential candidate Joe Biden. The New York Times cited Abrams as a major influence on boosting voter turnout in her state.

Stacey Abrams seems to believe that everyone should have a voice in American elections, which is a philosophy that falls very neatly in line with the overall egalitarian thinking behind "Star Trek." If anyone was to be present for Earth to rejoin the Federation, Abrams certainly fit the bill.

According to The Hollywood Reporter, Abrams has long been a fan of "Star Trek," and was well aware of the rise of Captain Michael Burnham. Abrams wanted to be involved in "Star Trek: Discovery" no matter what plotline was being explored. She posted a video to her Twitter/X account, and was quoted as saying:

"Whatever the story was, I wanted to be a part of it because watching Michael Burnham come into her power as captain, watching this party-crew reclaim itself — not only in a new era but in a new space that is so reminiscent of what they remembered but so different — that, to me, speaks of why we do the work we do in politics. [...] It's about giving people hope and giving them a better life and creating this vision of what is possible."

Abrams has long stood up for justice, fairness, and equality. One can find ample evidence online tracing her stances on issues like criminal justice reform, her belief in the power of public education, and her need to address her state's maternal health crisis. She possesses a progressive standpoint politically, which is fitting for the overall progressive attitudes of "Star Trek, so this was smart casting. As we mentioned in our own article ranking the best "Star Trek" guest stars, Abrams and "Star Trek" both remind us that the future can be better.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Dirk Kuyt celebrated a special night for Fenerbahce, as he believes the Turkish giants demonstrated what a "wonderful club" they are in their win over his old team Liverpool.

Kuyt spent six years at Liverpool, reaching the Champions League final with them in 2007.

Yet he lined up against the Reds on Wednesday, as he is in interim charge of Fener.

And the Dutchman led Fener to their first Champions League victory in 18 years as Vedat Muriqi's 79th-minute header clinched a 3-2 win.

Liverpool twice went ahead in Istanbul, through Trey Nyoni's first senior strike and Alexander Isak's maiden Champions League goal for the Reds.

Yet Fener twice fought back, with Mason Greenwood and Archie Brown on target before Muriqi's first Champions League goal settled the contest.

"It was a very special night for me and everyone who loves Fenerbahce," said Kuyt.

"We had some difficult times but I have to give a very big compliment to the players of Fenerbahce and also the staff members who keep pushing the players.

"We told each other to stick together, be united and keep believing in ourselves. That's something I learned from another club that means a lot to me and that is Liverpool, who we played tonight.

"I'm very happy with all the players, all the people involved, that we made a miracle tonight because not many people expected us to win. But we said before the game that we have a chance if we show passion, energy; if we stick to our game plan then the crowd will come behind us.

"We showed Europe what a wonderful club and what wonderful supporters that Fenerbahce has."

Fenerbahçe 3-2 Liverpool A fifth consecutive away defeat in the UCL for Liverpool against a Turkish club. Fenerbahçe were 2-1 down with just 17 minutes to go, but goals from Archie Brown & Vedat Muriqi turn the game around in their favour.

— Opta Analyst (@OptaAnalyst) November 4, 2026

Isak gave Liverpool a 2-1 lead in the 69th minute, meaning it is the latest they have ever gone ahead in a major European game and then lost.

Liverpool conceded three goals from set pieces in a Champions League game for just the second time, along with their 3-3 draw with Sevilla in November 2017, though this was the first time ever they have let in three non-penalty set-piece goals in one game.

"The main reason that I can find for why we lost this game is we conceded from three set pieces. It's something that we were doing very well in," he said.

"We knew it was one of the strengths of Fenerbahce. We concede from a free-kick, a corner and even from the long throw at the end.

"They're goals that have hurt us because we've gone twice in front after having good moments, controlling the game. We've found the goals but we have conceded in situations we should definitely defend better.

"It's frustrating because it's a difficult place to come here and play. In most parts of the game we were thinking we could win this one but obviously it hasn't happened.

"Especially the first goal and the third goal – the second one is a rebound – but the first one, I don't think we set the wall properly and the last one, Muriqi wins the ball against three defenders; it's definitely something we need to defend better."

OpenAI’s Enterprise Sales Take Majority as Brockman Sees IPO Readiness

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Reuters reported on November 5, 2026 that OpenAI President Greg Brockman said enterprise sales now generate the majority of the company’s revenue. Speaking on November 4, 2026, in Napa, California, Brockman answered “I think so” when asked whether OpenAI would be ready for an initial public offering in 2027.

The official conference program identifies Brockman as OpenAI’s co-founder and president, overseeing its products, computing efforts and business. His session, OpenAI’s Race to Scale, featured Wall Street Journal reporter Keach Hagey and was framed around AI infrastructure, ChatGPT’s business evolution, safety and cybersecurity, and future technology.

OpenAI’s business-sales effort includes promoting Codex as it competes with Anthropic for enterprise customers. Anthropic is likely to go public before the end of 2026, Reuters reported, making the rival’s expected listing timetable part of the context for Brockman’s answer.

OpenAI’s March 31, 2026 funding announcement supplies an earlier benchmark for the revenue mix. The company reported that enterprise accounted for more than 40% of revenue and was on track to reach parity with consumer revenue by the end of 2026, while the business overall was generating $2 billion in revenue per month. Brockman’s new description moves beyond that target: enterprise has shifted from a business expected to catch consumer to the larger contributor.

Codex supplies a product-level measure of the business push. OpenAI reported that the coding agent’s weekly user base had passed 2 million after expanding fivefold in three months, while activity was growing more than 70% month over month. That growth helps explain why Codex featured in Brockman’s account of competition for business customers.

The IPO answer also comes after OpenAI delayed its listing plans and accelerated fundraising to capitalize on recent growth. Its March announcement said the company had closed a round with $122 billion in committed capital at an $852 billion post-money valuation. Amazon, NVIDIA and SoftBank anchored the round, with continued participation from Microsoft. OpenAI also expanded its revolving credit facility to approximately $4.7 billion, and the facility remained undrawn at the round’s close.

Computing capacity connects that financing to the operating business. OpenAI’s March announcement described reliable computing access as a compounding advantage that supports model research and product development while increasing availability and reducing serving costs at scale. Its infrastructure portfolio spans several cloud providers, chip platforms and data-center partners, after expanding beyond a small number of core providers over the preceding 15 months.

Brockman’s answer expressed confidence about readiness without committing OpenAI to a 2027 listing. The central change is the business a potential offering would present: enterprise sales have moved beyond the earlier parity objective, while private funding and a broader infrastructure portfolio support a compute-heavy expansion. After the earlier IPO delay, the open question is whether OpenAI can convert that enterprise growth and compute investment into the public-market readiness Brockman described.
