What was really behind Wednesday's monster rally? Trump quickly making peace

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The stock market skyrocketed on Wednesday on the heels of a decisive sweep for Democrats in the 2026 midterm elections. But that was not necessarily the only driver of the climb.

"President Trump's acknowledgement of the Democrats' victory in the midterms and his expressed openness to collaborating with the opposition have helped markets to rally on hopes that contestation of the results and more adverse polarization can be avoided," said Thomas Hempell, Generali Investments senior economist.

Trump told The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal overnight Tuesday that he accepted the results and would work with Democrats. Those headlines coincided with a rebound in stock market futures.

In the lead-up to this year's midterms, some on Wall Street grew increasingly concerned about the prospect of Trump contesting the election results. The Department of Justice's Alaska election lawsuit led to fears over delays with the outcome that would exacerbate the uneasiness in the market that's typically removed once the elections conclude.

CNBC's Fed Survey showed that among 30 fund managers, strategists and economists, 63% believe that the extended uncertainty around the balance of power created by legal challenges to the election results posed "some" market risk. That was a slight increase from 61% in September. Additionally, 14% believed it represented "significant" market risk, which was up from 7%.

Now, investors are breathing a sigh of relief over Trump's acceptance of the results, which should "act as a break on extreme policy outcomes and bring down risk premia," so long as the president's decision remains, said George Saravelos, head of FX Research at Deutsche Bank.

Trump has a history of rallying markets, as well as driving them lower, with his announcements. Jay Woods of Freedom Capital Markets, who noted that the rebound in stocks overnight was partially due to the president's "initial tone and reaction to the results," said the focus will shift to whether Trump's latest move will actually have staying power.

"I think Trump is going to try to do and say the right thing, but at two in the morning, he may change his mind," the chief market strategist said. "He is an X factor."

Woods added that he's "encouraged" by Wednesday's massive rally, but he noted that there are still headwinds that could plague the market over the coming weeks — such as concerns about inflationary pressures as a result of higher energy prices from the conflict in the Middle East.

"How Trump reacts to it will help us navigate those headwinds," he said.

Osaic's Philip Blancato said he believes that if Trump "continues to play nice" with Democrats, the market is going to have a "very strong" end to the year.

Stocks historically tend to rally after the midterms anyway, but this time around, some were worried the solid run-up in the S&P 500 this year was going to potentially hinder those gains. After Trump's reaction, Blancato said he doesn't think the S&P 500 hitting 8,000 by year-end is totally out of the question and that there's "a lot predicated on him, but not completely."

The broad market index gained more than 1% on Wednesday, with its closing price implying a roughly 4% gain to that level.

However, investors should be prepared for any future volatility if Trump's rhetoric does not remain the same, the chief market strategist said.

"The president's always going to be a wildcard," he said. "Let's not let one day make a year. This is an instant reaction to this thing. There's still plenty of things to be hashed out."

Think of these backyard birds as your new mascot: They can sit on your porch, front yard, or terrace to offer cheerful charm for visitors and passersby. Five Hacks Every Una Watch User Should Know

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Una watch is pretty much made to be hackable, with its interchangeable parts and open development environment. But it’s also pretty barebones at launch, without many features available yet. However, here are five things I’ve discovered I can do with it—and I hope to expand this list quite a bit as the device and its community mature.

Because the Una watch has a regular USB-C port, you don’t need to remember any specific cable, dongle, or adapter to charge it. I knew that, but it didn’t occur to me until recently that I didn’t need a computer or a wall outlet to plug into the other end. I was able to take a USB-C to USB-C cable—the same kind I might carry around to charge my phone—and plug one end into my watch and the other into my phone. It charged!

(You can also charge some other devices from a phone, including an Apple Watch, but you’d need to carry around that specific adapter, which is why nobody bothers.)

The Una watch doesn’t have any built-in sleep tracking, but a few community-built apps aim to provide it. The Slumber app, for example, will let you know how long you slept and compare your most recent bedtime with when you usually get to bed. There are no sleep stages (honestly: refreshing, since watches are rarely good at this anyway), and the analysis is done on the watch itself.

But if what you’re really hoping for is a recovery score or an overnight HRV reading, the HabitLab app combines that with habit tracking. Every evening, the watch will buzz and ask you to check off which habits or factors played into your day: Alcohol and late meals are among the options, and the watch will automatically detect morning or evening workouts to add them to the list. In the morning, the watch asks you how you slept, and then it gives you a score based on your resting heart rate, HRV, and your subjective rating.

The app is supposed to be able to put together trends and insights from this data, but I haven’t managed to get it to do this. Maybe it’s waiting for more data (I’ve been using it a little over a week), or maybe it’s just buggy.

By contrast, the barcode app worked even better than I expected it to. It’s meant for a parkrun barcode; each participant in the free weekly 5K has a number that you can use to check your results online. But it works just as well for other barcodes.

I installed the Barcode app and entered the number for my library card. Perhaps because of the watch’s MIP screen, I found this barcode scanned more reliably than any barcodes I’ve previously tried to use on a watch. The library scanner picked it up just fine on the first try, and a phone-based scanner also had no problem with it. You can store up to six barcodes, so I’ve also entered a few others, like my grocery discount card.

The app supports QR codes as well as regular barcodes, and to enter a QR code, you just type in a shortened URL. (It needs to be 22 characters or less.) This means that the Barcode app is also a QR code creator, a nifty feature indeed.

Seeing how soon the sun sets is a bit of a downer as we leave daylight saving time and get into the dark winter months, but it’s also helpful in planning. The Sun & Moon Face puts a graphic on your wrist that shows how far along the sun is in its trajectory across the sky for the day. When evening is approaching, you’ll see just how little time you have left.

The app grabs your GPS location once a day, which means that for the first few hours I wore it, the sunrise and sunset times were incorrectly listed as 6 a.m. and 6 p.m. But as long as you aren’t flying to a drastically new location every day, the app does the job just fine. As a bonus, it changes the color of the screen’s sky to show daylight, nighttime, or sunrise/sunset colors.

If you want to hack this hackable watch, you might as well take the plunge and try to build something yourself. Instructions are here to download the development tools for Windows, Mac, or Linux, and there are tutorials for different features you might want to use and implement. There are also about a dozen example apps you can build on, including activity trackers, watch faces, and glances. I'm not much of a coder myself, but this is the friendliest setup I've seen for someone to get into building their own apps for a watch.

Report: Julian Ward willing to let Liverpool star go

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Julian Ward has a lot of decisions to make about Liverpool ahead of his first transfer window as their sporting director.

The Englishman took over the role from Richard Hughes after he chose to join Al-Hilal upon the completion of the 2026-27 summer transfer window.

Liverpool have already spent well over £150 million this season so, any other additions will need to be worthwhile.

Many different players have been linked with an Anfield switch but, with how poor Liverpool have been this term, they must make sure that every signing they agree is one that makes sense.

Even if that means some of their own players are going to have be put on the chopping block.

Liverpool are happy to let Federico Chiesa exit Anfield in January

Federico Chiesa is one of the most likely players to leave Liverpool this January.

The attacker has only been given a few minutes of playing time by Andoni Iraola and, since Arne Slot failed to give him all that much either, it's clear that the end of his time in L4 is upon us.

Of course, there have been some moments when he's stood out from the crowd but, he's failed to make enough of an impact to keep his place.

His exit has been discussed for some time now with Inter Milan being linked with the Italian's transfer once again.

"Inter are considering a move for Liverpool winger Federico Chiesa as they look to add a fifth forward to their squad in the January transfer window," Tribuna wrote.

"According to Tribuna.com sources, the Italian winger is emerging as one of the options being considered by the Nerazzurri. Chiesa has struggled for regular playing time at Liverpool and could be open to returning to Serie A.

"Liverpool would be willing to discuss a loan deal with an option to buy, with the permanent transfer fee set at around €8-10 million."

Liverpool must make sure they have enough depth in attack to sell Federico Chiesa

At the moment, most of Liverpool's injuries have happened to their attackers.

Cody Gakpo and Alexander Isak have both spent extended time on the sidelines this term and, while others such as Lewis Koumas and Rio Ngumoha are preferred over him, it's still nice for Liverpool to know that they have someone who's scored 83 goals across his career on their bench.

However, much like Slot, Iraola has chosen against giving Chiesa a decent amount of playing time so, it's clear that he's not in his immediate plans.

But, despite this, if a massive injury crisis occurs, the Reds need to be sure that they have enough firepower to continue shooting themselves up the table.

If not, they could go into free fall and end up even worse off than they were last term which is something that they certainly won't want to happen.

Anfield Watch is a long-running Liverpool FC fan site bringing you all the latest news from Anfield as well as transfer rumours, features, score updates, videos and interviews. Our journalists bring you content about the Reds 24 hours a day, 7 days a week.

The Art of the Panoramic Aspect Ratio

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Panoramic compositions offer another way to present a scene, preserving its horizontal breadth and giving the frame a different sense of scale, movement, and visual balance. They can be extremely powerful.

The proportions of a photograph influence how it is viewed. A conventional landscape frame provides a relatively balanced relationship between horizontal and vertical space, while a panoramic crop places considerably more emphasis on width. This changes the viewer’s experience, encouraging the eye to move across the image and take in the composition progressively.

That extended viewing experience is one of the format’s greatest strengths. Rather than concentrating attention within a relatively compact frame, a panoramic photograph gives visual information more room to unfold. The viewer can follow changes in light, examine relationships between foreground and background elements, and appreciate the full extent of the scene. This can make the photograph feel expansive without relying on dramatic processing or exaggerated perspective.

Panoramic images also have a distinctive sense of balance. Their proportions can create a feeling of openness and continuity, especially when the composition has a strong horizontal structure. The format can be quiet and understated or dramatic and imposing, depending on the subject, light, and arrangement of elements. It is not simply a way to include more of a scene. It changes which parts of the photograph receive emphasis and how the viewer moves through it.

The panoramic format works best when a scene’s horizontal breadth is an important part of its visual appeal. Landscape photography is an obvious application, but the format can also work well for architecture, cityscapes, environmental portraits, and other subjects where the surrounding space contributes to the image.

Before choosing a panoramic composition, consider what makes the original photograph compelling. If the subject depends on vertical elements or a strong relationship between foreground and sky, removing too much of the image’s height may weaken it. If the strongest visual information is distributed horizontally, however, a wider frame may create a more cohesive composition.

The format can also help simplify a photograph. Cropping away unnecessary sky or foreground reduces competing information and emphasizes the remaining elements. This is particularly useful when the original image contains substantial areas that contribute little to its visual impact. However, removing space simply to achieve a wider aspect ratio is not necessarily an improvement. Open areas can provide balance, establish scale, and give important details room within the frame.

It is worth comparing several aspect ratios before deciding on a final composition. A moderately wide image may preserve enough vertical information to maintain depth, while a more elongated format can create a stronger emphasis on horizontal movement. Neither is inherently better. The right proportions support the composition without sacrificing the information that matters.

A panoramic composition requires attention across the entire width of the frame. In a conventional photograph, the viewer may be drawn quickly toward a central subject. With a panoramic image, the extended proportions create more opportunity for visual movement, but they also make balance particularly important. A strong focal point should work with the rest of the frame rather than simply occupy the center.

Horizontal lines and directional elements can help establish that movement. The placement of the horizon, the alignment of natural or architectural features, and the distribution of contrasting areas all influence how the viewer moves through the photograph. Consider whether the composition has a clear visual starting point and whether the remaining elements support or interrupt that progression.

Negative space plays an equally important role. A wide frame does not need to be filled with detail from edge to edge. Open areas can provide visual relief, separate competing elements, and establish the atmosphere of the photograph. Removing too much space during cropping can make an image feel crowded, particularly when important subjects sit close to the boundaries.

Pay as much attention to the left and right edges as to the center. Small distractions become more noticeable when the composition is stretched horizontally, and an element that seems insignificant in the original frame may disrupt the finished image. Review the entire composition before releasing the shutter, and make small adjustments to camera position when necessary. The goal is not to fill every part of the frame, but to make each area feel intentional.

A wide aspect ratio can communicate a scene’s breadth, but width alone does not create depth. The relationship between elements at different distances remains essential. Overlapping forms, changes in contrast, and variations in light help establish separation and give the viewer a sense of how the scene extends into the distance.

Atmospheric conditions can be particularly effective in this regard. Haze, fog, rain, and changing light create tonal differences between near and distant elements. Preserving those transitions across a panoramic composition can give the photograph a strong sense of depth without requiring an exaggerated foreground.

Scale also benefits from thoughtful composition. Including recognizable elements within an expansive environment provides a reference point that helps viewers understand the size of the surrounding space. The challenge is to retain enough context for that relationship to be clear without allowing secondary details to compete with the main subject.

When editing, consider how much of the original foreground and background needs to remain. A panoramic crop that removes too much of either can flatten the photograph or change its sense of scale. The goal is to retain the relationships that give the image depth while letting the wider proportions emphasize the overall scene.

You can create panoramic photographs with almost any focal length. The choice depends on how much of the scene you need to include and how you want the different elements to relate to one another.

Wide-angle lenses capture a broad field of view and can preserve a strong relationship between foreground and background. They are useful when the composition depends on including substantial environmental context, although perspective becomes increasingly pronounced as the camera moves closer to foreground subjects. This can make nearby elements appear much larger in relation to the background.

Standard and telephoto lenses offer a different approach. Longer focal lengths isolate a narrower portion of the scene and can make distant elements appear more prominent within the composition. They are useful when the visual interest is concentrated in a particular section of the landscape or when the photographer wants to emphasize layers without including much foreground.

Camera position is just as important as focal length. Changes in height and direction can alter the alignment of elements, the amount of foreground visible, and the relationship between different parts of the scene. Take time to assess the composition before settling on a viewpoint, especially when working with a longer lens.

If the intention is to create a panoramic crop from a single exposure, leave sufficient room around important details. Cropping reduces the available resolution and may remove more of the original frame than expected. A little extra space gives you flexibility in editing and helps prevent important elements from being cut off.

There are two primary ways to create panoramic photographs: cropping a single exposure or stitching multiple frames together. Each approach has advantages, and the best choice depends on the scene, the available resolution, and whether anything within the frame is moving.

Cropping a single exposure is often the simplest option. It works particularly well when the camera’s native field of view already includes the necessary horizontal information and the resulting file retains enough resolution for the intended output. High-resolution cameras offer more flexibility, but it is still important to consider how much of the original image you’ll remove.

Stitching multiple photographs together can produce a much larger file with additional detail. This is useful when photographing a scene that extends beyond the camera’s field of view or when you intend to print a particularly large image. For consistent results, use a tripod when practical, keep the camera level, and overlap consecutive frames by approximately 25 to 30 percent. The overlap gives stitching software enough shared detail to align the images.

Manual exposure and a fixed white balance help maintain consistency throughout the sequence. If the camera changes exposure or white balance between frames, visible variations can appear in the finished image. Focus should also remain consistent. Consider focusing once and switching to manual focus before capturing the remaining frames, particularly when photographing distant subjects.

Stitching is not always the best choice. Movement within the scene can create alignment problems, duplicated details, or visible inconsistencies. Waves, foliage, clouds, and moving subjects may change position between exposures. In these situations, a single image may produce a more natural result. When stitching, also be mindful of nearby objects, since parallax can cause alignment errors if the camera rotates around the wrong point.

Post-processing shapes the final proportions and balance of a panoramic image. Most editing programs allow photographers to select a custom crop ratio or enter specific dimensions. Ratios such as 2:1 or 2.35:1 provide useful starting points, but there is no need to force every photograph into the same proportions.

Begin by identifying the visual information that needs to remain. Consider whether the sky, foreground, and surrounding details contribute to the photograph or simply occupy space. Adjust the crop gradually and compare different proportions. A relatively small change in height can have a significant effect on the image’s balance.

Check the position of the horizon and other prominent horizontal lines. Their placement should reflect the visual importance of the areas above and below them. Also review the edges for distractions, and make sure important details aren’t positioned awkwardly close to the boundaries.

For stitched panoramas, cropping also removes the uneven edges created during alignment. Leave enough room during capture to account for this, particularly when important details extend close to the edges of the frame. Once you’ve established the composition, inspect the full-resolution image for stitching errors, uneven transitions, and inconsistencies in exposure or color.

Avoid making the crop so tight that the image loses its sense of openness. One of the advantages of the panoramic format is the space it provides. Removing too much of that space can undermine the very quality that makes the format appealing.

Large prints are one of the most compelling applications of panoramic photography. The extended proportions make effective use of horizontal wall space, creating a presentation that feels distinct from a conventional landscape print. They are particularly well suited to areas where a standard rectangular photograph might appear too small or visually contained.

The viewing experience also changes with print size. A panoramic image encourages the viewer to move their attention across its length, taking in details and relationships that might be overlooked when viewing a smaller image on a screen. At a larger scale, subtle changes in tone, texture, and light become more apparent. The photograph can become something viewers return to rather than an image they take in at a glance.

The format is also versatile in interior spaces. Its proportions can work above furniture, along hallways, in offices, or across larger uninterrupted walls. A carefully composed panoramic print can provide a strong focal point without requiring an elaborate arrangement of multiple images. Its visual impact comes from the photograph itself and the space it occupies.

Preparing an image for a large print requires more consideration than simply selecting a wide crop. Resolution is particularly important. Cropping a single exposure reduces the available pixel dimensions, so check the final file against the print lab’s recommendations before ordering. If the intended print is especially large, stitching multiple frames may provide the additional resolution needed to retain fine detail.

The viewing distance should also inform the final output. A print intended to be seen from across a room does not necessarily require the same pixel density as one that will be examined from close range. Consult the printer’s specifications and consider the physical dimensions of the finished image before making final sharpening and output decisions.

The print surface influences how the photograph is presented. Matte papers reduce reflections and suit images with subtle tonal transitions, while luster and semi-gloss finishes add more surface sheen and can emphasize contrast. Canvas offers a different aesthetic, with visible texture and a softer rendering of fine detail. Choose a finish that complements the photograph rather than treating it as an afterthought.

Framing and presentation matter, too. Panoramic aspect ratios don’t always match standard frame sizes, so custom framing may be necessary. A mat can separate the photograph from its surroundings, while a frameless presentation gives the image a more continuous appearance. Consider the available wall space and the intended viewing distance when deciding on the final dimensions.

The strongest panoramic photographs aren’t necessarily the widest or most dramatic. Their success comes from the relationship between the subject, the composition, and the proportions of the finished image. The format should give the photograph a reason to extend horizontally, whether that means preserving the breadth of a landscape, emphasizing visual relationships, or creating a more immersive viewing experience.

When composing, look beyond the central subject and consider the entire width of the scene. Pay attention to balance, negative space, depth, and the placement of important details. Leave room to refine the crop, and consider the intended presentation before removing a substantial portion of the original image.

A panoramic photograph offers more than an alternative aspect ratio. It lets you control how a scene is experienced, giving the viewer space to explore the image and allowing the photograph to take on a distinctive presence when displayed at a larger scale.

How Leaders Can Turn Cultural Misunderstandings Into Stronger Teams

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

At my company, our differing backgrounds and perspectives make us stronger, but they can also lead to friction. Here’s how I handle it.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

“I don’t like him. He’s rude.” That was the chatter I was hearing about our newest hire, an IT whiz from Ankara in my native Turkey.

Dismayed as I was to hear this assessment, I wasn’t totally surprised. The person who made it was American; exactly the sort of eternally upbeat, outgoing person the country’s reputation is built upon. Our new hire, on the other hand, was more introverted and subdued — he was polite, but not overly focused on pleasantries.

What was happening here wasn’t a difference in personality as much as culture. I experienced this myself when I first moved to the U.S. — people were so friendly, it was almost unnerving. It took me a while to realize the friendliness wasn’t fake. Nor was it the same thing as closeness. Americans will ask how you’re doing, mean it for the few seconds it takes to respond and move on. In Turkey, you don’t ask unless you have the time to actually hear the answer. It took time for me to understand this and adapt to it.

Jotform has offices across five countries; even within those offices, our teams are incredibly diverse. Our strength comes from our differing backgrounds and perspectives, but they can also lead to moments of friction, like the one with our new hire. As leaders, it’s incumbent on us to build a culture that leverages these differences rather than suffers from them. Here’s how.

Hiring is one of the areas I refuse to delegate completely — it’s just too important. By the time we’ve narrowed the field to a handful of candidates for a position, it’s clear that any one of them has the qualifications needed to do the job well. But when making a final hiring decision, I want to learn more about how they think.

One of the key qualities I always look for? Open-mindedness.

Open-minded people don’t assume their default way of thinking is automatically the right one. When something collides with their perspective, they don’t dismiss it — they want to know more.

To find this, I like to ask candidates to tell me about a time they were proven wrong. Do they get defensive and shift blame, or do they walk me through what changed their mind? The answer tells me whether they’ll bring that same flexibility to a teammate who does things differently than they expect.

There are several reasons that I have my teams work in-office, not the least of which is that remote workers tend to struggle more with isolation and stress than onsite workers, and experience lower overall well-being.

That’s in part because so much is lost when we interact only through screens. Nonverbal communication, like body language and tone of voice, make up the bulk of how we present ourselves to others, much of which get flattened out online. If you recall the feeling of emptiness and exhaustion that often accompanied a pandemic-era online happy hour or team-building exercise, you know what I’m talking about.

At my company, we have what we call Jotformers Week, in which employees from nearly two dozen countries gather together for several days of camaraderie. And because no one likes forced fun, we ask what would make the experience meaningful for participants, ensuring everyone has a say in how the time is spent.

These retreats build a sense of cohesion, filling in the gaps that time and distance tend to create. A tone that seemed cold over Slack gets clarified over dinner. A silence that may have seemed disagreement turns out to be the way someone gathers their thoughts. Our American team quickly found out that our new Turkish IT hire only seems stern; he actually has a quietly wicked sense of humor that regularly has the group in stitches.

Making time for in-person team building is an expense, to be sure. But having your employees feel genuinely connected, even across oceans? That’s priceless.

In the absence of information, people will make it up, the saying goes. Ambiguity tends to breed misunderstandings, which can quickly erode the culture you’ve worked so hard to create.

Don’t leave room for misinterpretation. Write down what your company stands for, and make sure everyone sees it. Reinforce it regularly in visible forums like company-wide newsletters and all-hands calls.

Also, be clear about expectations. At Jotform, I encourage employees to sign off on nights and weekends. When they take PTO, I tell them to delete Slack off their phones and truly take time to refresh and recharge. This way, no one has to spend their family vacation stressing about whether they’re expected to check in — they already have the answer, no interpretation or guessing needed.

Not only does this practice create a sense of psychological safety, it also closes the gap where cultural misunderstanding tends to take root. The rule is just the rule, the same for everyone reading it.

Misunderstandings happen, especially when you have a culturally diverse team. Good leaders encourage their employees to approach differences with curiosity, create opportunities for team-building and reduce ambiguity.

“I don’t like him. He’s rude.” That was the chatter I was hearing about our newest hire, an IT whiz from Ankara in my native Turkey.

Dismayed as I was to hear this assessment, I wasn’t totally surprised. The person who made it was American; exactly the sort of eternally upbeat, outgoing person the country’s reputation is built upon. Our new hire, on the other hand, was more introverted and subdued — he was polite, but not overly focused on pleasantries.

What was happening here wasn’t a difference in personality as much as culture. I experienced this myself when I first moved to the U.S. — people were so friendly, it was almost unnerving. It took me a while to realize the friendliness wasn’t fake. Nor was it the same thing as closeness. Americans will ask how you’re doing, mean it for the few seconds it takes to respond and move on. In Turkey, you don’t ask unless you have the time to actually hear the answer. It took time for me to understand this and adapt to it.

Manchester United, Chelsea and Liverpool are reportedly interested in signing Eintracht Frankfurt defender Nnamdi Collins as Premier League clubs look to strengthen their defensive options.

The 22-year-old has attracted considerable attention with his performances in Germany, and several English clubs are closely monitoring his development ahead of future transfer windows.

According to a report from Fussball News, Manchester United, Chelsea and Liverpool are all keeping tabs on the German defender, having been impressed by his performances for Frankfurt and the German U21 national team.

The three English clubs are understood to have sent scouts to follow Collins, who has established himself as an exciting defensive prospect.

The German youngster offers considerable versatility, having shown he can play both centre-back and right-back. His adaptability could make him a useful addition for clubs looking to improve their defensive depth.

Furthermore, his impressive pace, physical strength and ability to compete in individual duels make him an attractive prospect for the Premier League. He has also caught scouts’ attention through his performances for the German U21 side, including their recent encounter against Georgia.

However, no concrete negotiations have taken place so far, and interested clubs are still assessing his suitability.

Meanwhile, Eintracht Frankfurt are unlikely to sanction his departure in the January transfer window. The Bundesliga outfit considers Collins an important part of their long-term plans and have secured his future with a contract running until June 2030.

Although his current market valuation is considerably lower, Frankfurt are reportedly looking for offers around €35 million before considering a sale.

The German club could also seek additional performance-related bonuses and a percentage of any future transfer fee.

It remains to be seen whether Manchester United, Chelsea or Liverpool will formalise their interest with an offer. However, Collins’ versatility and physical attributes make him an exciting defensive prospect, and a move to the Premier League could represent an important next step in his career.

How I learned to love an obnoxious phone grip

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Making the case for a loud smartphone accessory.

Making the case for a loud smartphone accessory.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Much like a great recipe, it started with a stick of butter.

This wasn’t real butter; it was a friend’s new phone grip, complemented by a phone case with bold stripes. Butter is in vogue now, or at least that’s what a 12-year-old told me a few months ago. This stick of butter was labeled “unsalty” and committed to the bit by including the appropriate tablespoon markings, just like the real thing. I was enamored. “Where did you get that?” I asked. It was a PopSocket-branded combo, and I found it so irresistible I ordered an equally eye-catching grip a few weeks later. After years of carrying buttoned-up, minimalist tech, I finally said, “To hell with it. I’m gonna have some fun.” I have zero regrets, and it seems I’m not alone in this journey.

Over the past few years I’ve been a vocal, occasionally regretful, member of Team No Phone Case. A case adds bulk and collects pocket lint. Why spend $800 on a piece of tech just to cover it up? If I must use a case, I want it to barely exist. This philosophy worked great with my iPhone 13 Mini, which is perfect as-is. Its size means it’s eminently grippable — no PopSocket necessary and it feels much more secure when I hold it. But phones are big now and are only getting bigger.

Since it’s just held on by a magnet, you can take it off whenever you want to present yourself as a serious grownup

An extended period using Google’s hefty Pixel 10 Pro Fold last year convinced me to adopt a magnetic grip just to use the thing more comfortably. At first, I gravitated toward the minimalist options like low-profile ring stands. But then I saw that stick of butter, and something clicked. A chunky grip is easy to hold. It’s fun. And since it’s just held on by a magnet, you can take it off whenever you want to present yourself as a serious grownup. Why not? I put in an order for a PopSocket shaped like a hot dog, and I was so excited when it arrived I accidentally pulled it apart.

This “why not have fun” ethos has clearly been part of Bailey Hikawa’s mission for the better part of a decade. She’s been making unconventional phone grips and cases out of her Los Angeles studio since 2018. Her designs incorporate input from the disabled community, aiming to offer an easier grip for people who hold their phone differently. But they can be appreciated by anyone who’s ever felt their phone slide out of their fingers. They’re also vibrant and bold. “I would like the phone case to feel like a friend or like a new stuffed animal,” she told The Verge in 2019. Earlier this year, Apple collaborated with Hikawa on a chunky, bright MagSafe grip. Her own online shop includes other grips made of flexible, clear urethane with spaghetti noodles or baby shrimp suspended inside. They’re handmade and the very definition of one-of-a-kind.

In more conventional territory, PopSocket has a surprising number of unusual grips and cases on offer. There’s a classic tomato pincushion, the food truck collection where I found my beloved hot dog, and an aptly named “Sugar Rush” collection. I asked PopSocket founder David Barnett whether I was alone in my pursuit of a bold grip. “We’re seeing more consumers embrace color, texture and personality in their phone accessories,” he told me over email. “I think people want their everyday essentials to feel a little less ordinary. Your phone goes everywhere with you, so why not have a little fun with it?”

I think phone makers are partly to blame here, too. Aside from your stray Cosmic Orange and anything Motorola is doing, phones mostly come in shades of Boring and Light Boring. Don’t even get us started on what passes for a purple phone these days. Maybe a radical reaction to this sameness was inevitable. Whatever the reason, you can find an awful lot of punchy, vibrant grips and cases without much effort. There are color-blocked “dopamine” cases all over TikTok and Amazon, viral phone cases that hold a tube of lip gloss, and OhSnap grips in colors called things like “hazmat honey” and “electric teal.”

Me and my ridiculous PopSocket are in good company. Or we will be — I left it in the office last time I was in New York and its absence has been bumming me out ever since. We’ll be reunited soon enough, but I guess if there’s one downside to being able to take a grip off your phone easily, it’s that you can accidentally leave it behind without much effort.

AMD's record quarter: Data center business doubles, while gaming craters

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

AMD’s gaming revenue fell 40 percent in an otherwise record $13.4 billion quarter.

AMD reported third-quarter revenues on Tuesday with a record $13.4 billion, an increase of 45 percent year over year, as data center business nearly doubled and the company’s gaming segment cratered.

AMD CFO Jean Hu said, “We expect revenue to be approximately $17 billion, plus or minus 3%. The middle point of our guidance represents approximately 66% year-over-year revenue growth, primarily driven by the acceleration of our Data Center segment.” AMD says its Q4 revenue projections are up another 27 percent from this quarter’s results. As reported by Tom’s Hardware, Intel’s Q4 guidance places revenue estimates between $15 billion and $16 billion. That means that if both companies land where they expect to at the end of the fourth quarter, AMD would exceed Intel’s revenue for the first time in history.

The data center segment of AMD’s business was up 97 percent year over year, with a record $8.5 billion. AMD attributes the growth to demand for its EPYC line of enterprise server CPUs, along with the launch of the Instinct MI450 series GPUs. Sales of AMD Ryzen mobile chips led growth in the Client business, which includes desktop Ryzen processors and posted $2.95 billion in revenue, up 7 percent year over year.

Gaming revenue fell sharply for the company, posting a 40 percent drop in revenue at $773 million, which AMD attributes to “lower semi-custom and graphics revenue.” Operating margins in AMD’s data center business expanded from 25 percent to 34 percent, with operating income surging 168 percent to $2.88 billion.

Credit: AMD Credit: AMD

Embedded segment revenue, or all the SoCs, FPGAs, and boards under AMD’s umbrella that go into other hardware, was $1.1 billion, up 29 percent. AMD attributed this growth to gains made in the “aerospace and defense, automotive, communications, and industrial end markets.”

Credit: AMD Credit: AMD

The gaming segment of AMD’s business is composed of two main sources. There are semi-custom processors that AMD supplies to other manufacturers’ consoles, as well as its own Radeon graphics business. Where things can get confusing is that Ryzen processors, like those commonly installed in gaming PCs, are counted under the company’s client business, not gaming. According to AMD Chair and CEO Lisa Su, “In Gaming, revenue declined 40% year over year to $773 million, primarily due to lower semi-custom sales as expected at this stage of the console cycle.” The financial results do not break down the split between console chip sales and PC graphics cards.

Credit: AMD Credit: AMD

Along with the Q3 financial results, AMD used the occasion to highlight hardware still in the hopper. The new AMD Threadripper Halo Station, which AMD first showed at IFA in September, pairs a Threadripper 9995WX CPU with up to four Instinct MI350P GPUs and can run trillion-parameter LLMs locally. The system is slated for a release sometime in 2027, with pricing still to be announced.

AMD also announced that Windows-based systems running on its Ryzen AI Halo platform will be “coming soon.” Known as Project Zenith, the effort will support a developer-focused Windows experience on the current PRO 400 Series platform. Built on the Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 processor, the chip supports up to 192GB of unified system memory, with up to 160GB of that available to be allocated for graphics, and is capable of running 300 billion-parameter models at 4-bit quantization. On the call, Su stated that AMD “started shipping MI455X in volume in the quarter, and lead customers, including Anthropic, Meta, Microsoft, OpenAI, and Oracle, brought their first Helios racks online quickly.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

China Unveils Their Plan for a Crewed Moon Landing

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Yesterday, on November 3rd, the deputy chief designer of China's crewed space program and chief designer of the lunar exploration program (Wang Xiang) unveiled an official statement detailing its plans for a crewed mission to the Moon. Under the proposed architecture, China will conduct two launches using the Long March 10 rocket, sending the Lanyue lunar lander and the Mengzhou crewed spaceship to the Moon, where they will rendezvous in orbit. Of the three taikonaut crew, two will then transfer from the spaceship to the lander and descend to the surface to conduct science operations.

The plans were outlined in a keynote speech Wang delivered at the 7th Manned Space Symposium, held from Oct. 31st to Nov.1st at Beihang University in Beijing. This year's theme was "Gathering Wisdom for the Galaxy: Digital and Intelligent Empowerment for High-Quality Development of Manned Spaceflight," and saw more than 800 experts and scholars from fields related to crewed spaceflight convene to discuss strategies, technologies, and the hurdles China's space program will face.

These plans echo the preliminary plan statements made at the 9th China (International) Commercial Aerospace Forum in July 2023. As Deputy chief engineer Zhang Hailian of the China Manned Space Engineering (CMSE) office said at the time, two Long March-10 rockets would launch the elements to the Moon separately. Zhang's slideshow also included a rendering of a lander depicted on a mural inside China's Manned Lunar Deep Exploration Project Office.

The CMSE unveiled the lander design to the world in September 2023 and officially named it Lanyue ("embracing the Moon") in early 2024. Since then, development of the Long March 10, Mengzhou spacecraft, and Lanyue lander has proceeded apace and reached several key milestones. This included a tethered test of the lander last year, which successfully simulated descent, guidance, control, and engine shutdown.

The Mengzhou spacecraft also successfully completed a series of zero-height abort and maximum dynamic pressure escape tests in June 2025 while the Long March-10 successfully completed a low-altitude demonstration and validation test earlier this year. These developmental milestones place China on track to complete a crewed lunar mission by 2030.

In an interview with China's state-controlled media company, CCTV, Wang said that their planning approach follows two principles and will consist of using different vehicle variants for different stages. To prepare for rendezvous in lunar orbit, China will use the Tiangong space station in Low Earth Orbit (LEO) to validate the rockets, lander and space capsule. An uncrewed flight mission of the Lanyue lander will also happen before a crew landing is attempted, which will gather critical environmental data on the luanr surface.

In addition, Wang highlighted how in preparation for crewed missions, the mission components will all be tested in an uncrewed state first:

We will use the near-Earth version of the Long March 10 to launch the near-Earth version of the Mengzhou spacecraft to perform space station missions in Low-Earth Orbit, validating the rocket's basic technologies and the critical re-entry and return phases of the spacecraft. Then we will use the lunar version of the Long March 10 to launch the lunar version of the Mengzhou spacecraft to execute the lunar landing mission. This is the validation process for the rocket: near-Earth first, then the Moon.

Artist's recreation of the two Chinese spacecraft that will put 'taikonauts' on the Moon. Credit: CSM

For the near-Earth phase of the plan, the Long March 10A (CZ 10A) will be used, a single stick variant with only two stages and no boosters. For the lunar mission, the standard, three-stage variant with additional boosters will be used, which has a payload capacity of 27 metric tons (~30 U.S. tons). This process is virtually identical to what NASA has been pursuing with its Artemis Program. Initially, NASA hoped to use the Block 2 variant of the Space Launch System (SLS) to deliver crew and payloads to the lunar surface (which has since been cancelled).

China's incremental "near-Earth first, then the Moon" approach is also very similar to the NASA's current launch plans. Before a crewed Orion spacecraft flew four astronauts around the Moon with Artemis II, NASA launched the uncrewed Artemis I capsule on a circumlunar flight to validate all the key systems. China's stated plans for an orbital rendezvous is also similar to NASA's revised plans for the Artemis III mission scheduled for next year, involving the seperate launch and an Orion spacecraft with a yet-to-be-determined Human Landing System (HLS).

"In an uncrewed state, we will validate many key technologies of the entire crewed process as possible, including launch, orbiting the Moon, docking with the Mengzhou spacecraft, landing and staying on the lunar surface, taking off again, and re-docking with the Mengzhou spacecraft," Wang added. "This is to ensure that the crewed flight is safe and reliable." Wang further indicated that the program has established system plans, technical standards and quality requirements under a single development framework.

China's long-term vision includes the creation of an International Lunar Research Station, the construction of which is currently planned to commence in 2031 using the super heavy-lift Long March 9 rockets. Roscosmo's involvement in the project is unclear at this time due to Russia's ongoing war in Ukraine and the way this has led to record lows in terms of annual launches and delays in the super-heavy Yenisei rocket. The details of this base are sitll somewhat vague, with the stated description claiming it will be located on the surface or in orbit.

Further Reading: CASC, Beihang University

Matt Williams is a space journalist, science communicator, and author with several published titles and studies. His work is featured in The Ross 248 Project and Interstellar Travel edited by NASA alumni Les Johnson and Ken Roy. He also hosts the podcast series Stories from Space on Patreon. He lives in beautiful British Columbia with his wife and family. For more information, check out his website.

The contest at home to Enfield on Saturday was disrupted early on when a kit clash between the sides became apparent. Just five minutes in, play was halted while officials tried to resolve the issue, with Hill switching to white shirts.

The visitors wasted little time in taking control. Thomas Avgoustidis opened the scoring in the 15th minute before Amrit Bansal-McNulty doubled their advantage 10 minutes later.

Goalkeeper Roco Rees produced several important saves and Hill manager Gary Mansell made two changes at half-time in an attempt to turn the contest around, introducing Charlie Lambert and Ben Pope.

On Tuesday night, luck was not on the Hillians’ side as they conceded an agonisingly late equaliser at Wingate & Finchley, despite an exceptional display of hard work and resilience. The performance was a clear improvement on Saturday’s heavy home defeat.

The Hillians started the first half on the front foot, and this positive approach continued throughout the opening 45 minutes as they looked the more likely side to break the deadlock.

They produced some excellent passages of play, creating several promising half-chances for Ben Pope and Charlie Lambert, with the latter coming closest to opening the scoring. With five minutes remaining in the first half, Pope was shown a yellow card. Moments later, the half-time whistle sounded with neither side able to find the breakthrough.

As the second half got underway on a cold evening in North London, the Hillians continued to take the initiative, creating further opportunities to open the scoring.

Lambert came close before Yoan Edoukou flashed a shot across goal from inside the box, which agonisingly went wide of the far post just before the hour mark.

As the half progressed, Roco Rees was called into action just before the 75-minute mark, producing an excellent save to keep the scores level. Shortly afterwards, Chris Whelpdale was introduced in place of Charlie Lambert.

Then, with 80 minutes played, the Hillians won a corner, which was delivered by Edoukou towards the near post. Whelpdale, with his first touch, connected brilliantly with a header that sailed over the goalkeeper and into the net, giving the Hillians a deserved lead.

Following the goal, Burgess Hill defended resolutely, with Harry Lawson producing a last-ditch block to prevent the hosts from drawing level.

With 90 minutes played and a minimum of six minutes of added time indicated, the Hillians made another substitution, with Jake King replacing Yoan Edoukou. However, with virtually the last action of the game, the hosts were awarded a penalty, which Ryan Lowe converted to make it 1-1 at full time.

It was heartbreak for the Hillians, who had been the better side throughout and looked deserving of all three points, only to leave with just one.

Nevertheless, there were plenty of positives to take from the performance, particularly following Saturday’s result. The hard work, determination and resilience shown by the players were there for all to see, and the boys should take great pride in their efforts on this occasion.

As always, a huge thank you goes to the travelling Hillians supporters who made the journey to North London. Your tremendous support is, as ever, greatly appreciated.

Next up for the Hillians is another away trip on Saturday, when they travel to Chatham Town FC for a 3pm kick-off.

Colorful 1,800-year-old belt buckle discovered in a Ukrainian forest may have belonged to a nomadic equestrian warrior

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A Roman-era belt buckle discovered in a Ukrainian forest hints at the ancient conflict between the Romans and the Dacians.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Archaeologists in Ukraine have found a colorful belt buckle that may have been worn by a nomadic equestrian warrior 1,800 years ago on the fringes of the Roman Empire.

The belt buckle was unearthed by local residents in the Tsetsyn Forest in southeastern Ukraine and given to Antonii Moisei, a professor at Bukovinian State Medical University, according to a translated statement. The roughly square metal buckle, which is about 0.8 inches (2.1 centimeters) on each side, has teal, blue and yellow enamel decorations.

The style of the decoration and the metal construction place the creation of the buckle in the second to third centuries A.D., when this part of Ukraine was in the ancient province of Roman Dacia, according to Moisei.

The column that the Roman emperor Trajan erected in Rome following the empire's triumph in the Dacian Wars.

Under Emperor Trajan, Rome fought the Dacian Wars (A.D. 101-102 and 105-106) against the denizens of the Dacian kingdom and its leader, Decebalus. Roman legions destroyed Dacia and annexed it as a new province. Trajan celebrated his victory in Rome, complete with a famous column that depicts details of the Dacian Wars.

In addition to the buckle, Moisei collected a decorative belt fitting and three small, round metal objects that probably belonged to the same belt. Moisei created a reconstruction of what the belt might have looked like before it was lost in the forest nearly two millennia ago.

But it's unclear who lost the belt. One of Moisei's theories is that the belt belonged to a horse-riding Sarmatian warrior originally from the Black Sea region. The Sarmatians were a group of tribes that lived outside the borders of the Roman Empire, in what are now central Ukraine, southern Russia and the Balkans. While some Sarmatian warriors fought against the incursion of the Roman Empire, others fought with Rome as mercenaries.

The metal belt fragments are part of a Roman provincial craft tradition, according to the statement, and they may have been obtained through trade or as a trophy of war. But Moisei is still studying the artifacts to confirm whether they were Sarmatian. Either way, the military belt provides new evidence of Roman military contact on the outskirts of the empire, and future work could help experts understand how the Romans interacted with the local Dacians during wartime.

From Augustus to Nero, see how much you know about ancient Rome's famous leaders with our Roman emperor quiz!

Kristina Killgrove is a staff writer at Live Science with a focus on archaeology and paleoanthropology news. Her articles have also appeared in venues such as Forbes, Smithsonian, and Mental Floss. Kristina holds a Ph.D. in biological anthropology and an M.A. in classical archaeology from the University of North Carolina, as well as a B.A. in Latin from the University of Virginia, and she was formerly a university professor and researcher. She has received awards from the Society for American Archaeology and the American Anthropological Association for her science writing.

Daniel Sikkema Sentenced to Life in Prison for the Murder of New York Art Dealer Brent Sikkema

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Daniel Sikkema has been sentenced to life in prison for his role in the murder of his estranged New York art dealer husband, Brent Sikkema, the United States District Court for the Southern District of New York announced on Wednesday.

Daniel Sikkema was found guilty in May of charges tied to a murder-for-hire plot of Brent Sikkema that took place in Rio de Janeiro in January 2024. After a two-week trial in New York, Daniel was convicted of three charges: murder-for-hire, conspiracy to commit murder-for-hire, and conspiracy to commit murder in a foreign country. Related Articles Jury Convicts Daniel Sikkema in Killing of New York Dealer Brent Sikkema Trial Begins in Brent Sikkema Murder-For-Hire Case

“Today’s life sentence holds Daniel Sikkema accountable for a cold, calculated murder-for-hire plot that resulted in the murder of his husband, Brent Sikkema,” said US Attorney Jamie McDonald in a statement, adding that for months Daniel Sikkema had “manipulated friends, family, and employees to execute a killing driven by greed” and that his actions had demonstrated a “profound disregard for human life and the rule of law. This sentence reflects the seriousness of the crime, and it reflects the strength and perseverance of the victim’s community and our law enforcement partners.”

Brent Sikkema was stabbed to death 18 times in Brazil at age 75 on January 14, 2024, which prosecutors argued was planned following a bitter divorce between the couple and a custody dispute involving their son. Brent had founded his gallery in 1991, which was known for its early support of artists like Kara Walker, Vik Muniz, Jeffrey Gibson, and Sheila Hicks. (Following his death, Sikkema Jenkins & Co. was renamed Sikkema Malloy Jenkins.)

To orchestrate the murder, Daniel hired Alejandro Prevez to carry out the act, promising to send Prevez and a romantic interest money; Daniel sent 11 payments in total. Shortly after Brent’s murder, Daniel “secretly sent a $5,000 payment to Prevez for the murder,” according to court files. Prevez was arrested by Brazilian law enforcement on January 18, 2024, and his criminal case remains pending in Brazilian courts.

Harriet Phillips Says Raising Her Daughter Solo Was “Painful.” But Now She Can Ask Kate Middleton for Advice

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Harriet Phillips, the wife of Peter Phillips, son of Princess Anne, sat down with Hello! for an exclusive interview five months after her royal wedding, which took place at All Saints’ Church in Kemble, Gloucestershire, the village where she grew up. The ceremony was attended by King Charles III and Queen Camilla, as well as many other members of the royal family.

For Harriet, who until then led a life far removed from high society, her official entry into one of the world’s most famous royal families inevitably brought about some big changes.

Harriet and Peter Phillips at Royal Ascot

She and her daughter, Georgina, now live at Gatcombe, Princess Anne’s estate. Peter’s sister, Zara Tindall, also lives there with her husband, Mike, and their three children. After 24 years with the British National Health Service (NHS), where she specialized in child mental health and caring for the most vulnerable children, Harriet left the NHS to embark on a new professional venture at a private foundation, continuing to focus on early-childhood development.

She has also taken on her first official role as patron of Home-Start Cotswolds, an organization that supports families with young children. “Becoming a parent can be one of the most wonderful experiences in life, but it can also feel overwhelming and isolating,” Phillips told Hello! “We often assume that parenting comes naturally, but the reality is that everyone needs support at different points. Home-Start offers vital, practical, and nonjudgmental support. Just having someone to listen can make an enormous difference.”

For Harriet, the decision to leave the health service does not represent a break with the past: “It’s been an enormous privilege to spend 24 years working in the NHS, and I’ve loved my career. It has taught me so much, professionally and personally. Over time, I’ve become increasingly passionate about the importance of promoting healthy brain development. If we can support parents during this critical early period, we have an opportunity to make a lasting difference for children.”

The Phillips couple at the 2026 DMMI Royal Charity Polo Cup

Harriet then looked back on her love story with Peter, which began in an entirely unexpected way. The two first met properly in early 2022, when their three daughters were on the same hockey team. At first there was just some casual small talk between them, then walks with their dogs, followed by dinners at pubs away from prying eyes and, finally, evenings spent cooking together. A low-key courtship, away from the spotlight, blossomed into a love story.

When she met Peter, there wasn’t a “thunderbolt moment”; Harriet said she simply felt comfortable with him, enjoying an unspoken sense of calm. “I just felt incredibly at ease with him and experienced a lovely, peaceful, safe feeling when talking to him—like being with a really good friend,” she told Hello!

He was the one who made the first move: “Peter was the brave one in recognizing that there was something between us and acting on it. This time, I thought, If I’m lucky enough to meet someone again, I want things to move slowly and ensure that everything is entirely right.” Then she added, unable to hide her happiness: “He is very romantic, loving, kind, loyal, amazing, and my best friend.” Their early dates were simple and low-key. “He’s a great cook and I love cooking. He’s great at barbecues. It was wholesome and suited to who we both are as people,” she said.

On July 25, 2025, during a weekend in the south of England, Peter proposed on a beach. “We were on a weekend getaway in the south of England on a hot day during the heat wave. He asked me to marry him on the beach; I was completely overcome,” she said. “He had the ring made at Pragnell beforehand—it’s absolutely beautiful.” Neither of their families had been told about the surprise: “It was the best-kept secret ever.”

During the interview, Harriet also reflected on her childhood in Gloucestershire and fondly remembered her parents and their long marriage. “I was brought up in a village in the middle of fields. My parents celebrated an incredible 51 years of marriage, and sadly, my father died just before Peter and I got together.” It was an experience that left a deep mark on her life, and today, as she said in reflecting on her own love story, Harriet also sees it in the context of meeting the Princess Royal’s son: “I often think maybe that’s why Peter came along.”

The loss of her father, and the difficulties she faced in the years that followed, helped shape her outlook on life. Harriet explained: “There is a beauty that comes out of difficult situations. A beautiful jewel is formed under pressure. Difficulties in life enable you to relate to people and build empathy.”

Harriet and Peter with Princess Royal Anne

Harriet then talked about her first meeting with Princess Anne, her eventual mother-in-law, which was much more informal than she had imagined it would be. They met in the garden at Gatcombe, sitting on benches and drinking cups of tea. “There was always going to be initial nerves about meeting anyone’s family members, but I am quite a calm person,” she said. “It was really relaxed…It couldn’t have been nicer.”

Harriet has come to appreciate, above all, the princess’s dedication to her work and local charities: “She sets an amazing example with her work ethic. What I’ve seen is her tireless attention to local charities and her genuine interests. It’s important to do things that match who you are, where you live, and what your passions are.”

And their relationship already seems very strong. “She’s brilliant, really good fun, and straight-talking,” Harriet said. “She’s incredibly generous—we had our wedding reception at Gatcombe, and it was amazing and special for Peter and I to have our wedding where he grew up.”

Charles and Camilla also welcomed her warmly right from the start, as seen in the various photos taken at public events. Her first meeting with the king and the queen consort took place at Royal Ascot: “It was so lovely and relaxed and there was lots of laughter. You feel as though you’re meeting family, but at the same time, it feels otherworldly. Both the king and the queen have been so welcoming to me and Georgina. I can’t thank them both enough.”

The couple with the king and queen at Royal Ascot

After their wedding, Harriet and Peter attended several public events, including Wimbledon. In September, they were together at Somerset House in London for the opening of the exhibition “Reviving Craft: Reflection of the Mind,” dedicated to traditional Chinese craftsmanship and featuring over 70 artists.

Harriet also spoke with Hello! about Kate Middleton, including the admiration she feels for her and the bond they’ve formed. “I can look to Catherine for advice and guidance,” she said. She recalled a special moment during a vacation on the Isles of Scilly: The children (Georgina, George, Charlotte, and Louis) found themselves playing in the water together, in a spontaneous and informal atmosphere. “I’ve had an incredible experience being welcomed in. We happened to be on holiday in the same place. It was great because the children were all jumping into the water at the same time,” she shared. “It was very casual, but there is a clear level of respect there.” And speaking of the Princess of Wales, she added: “She’s very openhearted, and I would always feel comfortable asking her if I needed advice about something.”

Her first stay at Balmoral with her daughter was also a special experience. Scotland is one of Harriet’s favorite destinations, and vacations with the royal family have remained surprisingly simple: “It’s the loveliest family holiday with an extraordinary layer…. We go walking and running, play football with the children, cycle in the hills, and go wild swimming, which is fantastic.”

Then, inevitably, her memories turned to the wedding, an event marked by emotional moments. Harriet got ready with her mother and the three girls in the family: Georgina, Savannah, and Isla. Then her brother, Nicholas, arrived to drive her to the church. “It was quite wet and it had rained that morning, but the girls kept my train completely white and immaculate,” said said. “They did a really good job.”

In the church, decorated with floral arrangements that seemed to bring nature indoors, Harriet carried a bouquet with myrtle and lily of the valley: “All my family members have had myrtle in their wedding flowers, so I continued that tradition.”

Before going in, the couple prayed together with the vicar. “Then [we] walked into the fragrance of the flowers in the church. It was incredible,” she said. “It’s such a beautiful church, and I grew up going to it. It’s tiny—we were all squished in!” The presence of the king, the queen, and other members of the royal family didn’t distract her: “I was so focused on being in the moment. Of course, you’re aware that people are around you, but I really wanted to enjoy it and be present. I was so happy. That’s very natural for me. I was peaceful. I prayed. Maybe the peace of God came over me.”

The reception took place in the garden at Gatcombe. The sun came out at one point; there was live music, Champagne, and a casual menu featuring lamb raised on the estate. Instead of the traditional wedding cake, there was a huge tiramisu. “There was lots of laughter and it was an amazing family day. It finished at midnight, but everyone wanted it to carry on,” she said.

Before meeting Peter, Harriet was a single mother raising her preteen daughter, Georgina. It wasn’t always an easy journey, which made her particularly sensitive to the challenges that parents face and the importance of having a support network.

“Although at times it was a painful experience, I did have the support of people who loved us. Any time in life when you’re under pressure or facing adversity, something good always comes from it,” she said. “Lean in; don’t be scared.” Her Christian faith played an important role—“It’s definitely the foundation of how I live; it’s like a golden thread that has woven through my life. I was brought up in a Christian family, but this is a little bit different because it’s like making a decision for yourself: It is a personal relationship and a conscious choice, rather than just being religious.”

Harriet is very proud of her daughter and the relationship they’ve built. “Becoming a mother gives you an inner strength to protect your loved one and face whatever comes,” she said. “Everyone has challenges in life that are dressed up differently. Maybe this has been my challenge—the experience of bringing up a child on my own. I’ve been able to see the good things that have come out of it.”

Harriet and Peter with their three daughters on their wedding day

Today her family has grown, and Harriet looks to the future with enthusiasm, but without rushing: “Peter and I are so fortunate to have such wonderful girls between us. I’m excited about what’s to come with our family and always mindful that going gently is so important.”

Her new life with Peter doesn’t mean setting aside her own identity. “I will continue as I am. I’m a professional and will continue working in the area I’m passionate about,” she said. “I’ve got a wealth of knowledge that I don’t want to waste, so I will continue to use that.” When asked what she’d like to accomplish in the next 12 months, she gave an answer that once again came back to children and families: raising greater awareness of the work of Home-Start Cotswolds. As she explained, “We don’t just support babies by caring for them directly—we support babies by supporting the people who care for them.”

It’s a philosophy that also seems to capture her new sense of balance in her life. She has joined the royal family without ceasing to be Harriet, the woman who for 24 years worked alongside children and their parents, and who today has found in Peter a life partner and, above all, a best friend.

Her mission at Home-Start brings her even closer to the Princess of Wales, who has also been committed for years to supporting the well-being of children in their early years, a crucial period that shapes their personalities and determines the adults they will become. Who knows if the two women, now close friends, might one day form a professional partnership as well.

Harriet with Peter, Zara, and Mike Tindall.

Nabrawind’s Crane-Free Wind Towers Face a Supplier Test

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Spain’s Nabrawind has developed a way to install exceptionally tall wind turbine towers without bringing an enormous crawler crane to the site. Its Nabralift system uses a tubular upper tower supported by a three-legged steel structure. Hydraulic equipment raises the assembled turbine while modular sections are inserted below it near ground level. The lifting frame can then be removed and used on another installation. This addresses genuine logistical and construction problems, particularly at remote sites where specialist cranes are expensive to transport and where wind can interrupt conventional lifting operations.

The company has progressed beyond demonstration-scale hardware. In September 2026, UL Solutions issued type certification for Envision’s 7.8 MW turbine on Nabrawind’s 188-meter tower. Fortescue is using 17 of those tower configurations at the 133 MW Nullagine wind project in Western Australia’s Pilbara region, where construction is underway. Nabrawind previously erected a 160-meter prototype in Spain in under two weeks and has supplied smaller commercial towers in Morocco and France.

The engineering benefits are credible. Nabrawind reports using roughly 20% less tower steel than comparable conventional tubular designs and reducing foundation concrete by as much as 80%, depending on the design and site. Those are supplier claims rather than independently established savings across all wind farms. The potential to avoid large cranes, simplify transport and raise turbines in windier conditions could also matter substantially to total installed cost and construction schedules. High hub heights only earn their keep when those costs are justified by the electricity generated over the project’s life.

The current deployment has an unusual commercial structure. Fortescue acquired Nabrawind in September 2025 and is building Nullagine to serve its own mining operations. Fortescue has the capital, internal electricity demand and industrial incentive to support a technology it owns. Successful installation in that context would be important evidence of construction performance, but it would not establish that an unrelated wind developer can finance and maintain the same system under ordinary third-party commercial arrangements.

Nabralift’s proprietary construction approach creates a procurement issue. Conventional tubular towers can be sourced from several qualified manufacturers, and experienced contractors can compete for much of their construction work. A developer selecting Nabralift currently depends on one supplier for the core tower system, specialist lifting knowledge and associated support. Wind turbine original-equipment manufacturers also create supplier dependencies, but concentrating another critical part of the project in a proprietary installation system requires its own contract and risk assessment.

A type certificate provides valuable technical assurance about the certified turbine-and-tower configuration. A lender or insurer still needs enforceable delivery schedules, long-term warranties, repair and maintenance provisions, clarity on replacement parts, and a workable remedy if the supplier’s capacity or commercial priorities change. These questions become more important when a wind farm must operate for 25 years or longer and there is no qualified alternative provider of the same system.

Nabrawind’s next important proof point is an independently developed and financed large wind farm that accepts those risks on commercial terms. Qualified installation partners, credible long-term service arrangements and greater competitive capacity could make its engineering advantages easier for developers to buy. The 188-meter tower has earned technical attention; its broader market depends on financing and procurement confidence catching up with the machinery.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Nabrawind’s self-erecting design, Fortescue’s Nullagine deployment and the supplier concentration that independent wind developers would have to manage. It follows my earlier assessment of 300-meter wind towers, where construction economics were the central challenge.

Laura Loomer, Elon Musk, George Santos, and More React to Election Night: “I Hope Everyone Is Learning Arabic”

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The midterm elections marked a night of historic firsts: Adam Hamilton became the first Democratic senator elected in Kansas in nearly 100 years; Chris Pappas is now the first openly gay man to be voted into the Senate; Keisha Lance Bottoms is the first Black woman to be elected as a US governor; Bentley Hudgins is the first transgender and nonbinary lawmaker to be voted into the Georgia General Assembly—the list goes on.

How better to chronicle a generational evening in US history than on social media? Our founding fathers recounted watershed moments via post riders and printed broadsides; in the mid-19th century, dispatches were sent via the Pony Express. President Lincoln’s shooting was rushed to the public via Morse code tapped over the wires, and during World War I, we utilized undersea telegraph cables. Yet in our long history spanning radio broadcasts, satellite television, and smoke signals, nothing has quite captured the zeitgeist like an X post.

“CNN is now saying the national anthem has been changed to a reggaeton cover of Pink Pony Club,” tweeted one user last night, right as the media joined a chorus of policy analysts and pundits to announce a new dawn as Democrats took control of Congress—a blue wave. Or rather, a blue tsunami.

Social media users across the country then took to X to mourn, deny, or celebrate the victory. Yet amid the cacophony of social media’s tweets, posts, replies, comments, and reels, the most notorious influencer grew shockingly quiet: President Donald Trump himself. Despite posting a half-hearted image of a MAGA-red hat reading “VETO,” Trump has not said much online in the hours following the victory.

In the months leading up to the midterms, social media did more than simply sway victories. In fact, it propelled candidates like Jon Ossoff to the forefront through short-form vertical videos, made contenders such as Abdul El-Sayed go viral through clips set to songs like Eminem’s 2010 hit “Not Afraid,” and directed Gen Z focus to key races through memes (“TEXAS JUST WENT BLUE. PUT PRONOUNS BACK IN YOUR BIO NOW,” New York City Council member Chi Ossé posted in his Instagram Stories last night).

More than three quarters of young adults relied on social media or platforms like YouTube as a source for their midterm election news this year. An astounding 67% of millennials also got the lowdown from content creators. Call it absurd, call it brain rot, or call it sensational: There’s no doubt that social media has become a phenomenon, and the Twitterverse is still alive and well in 2026.
