Hey, Siri: Plug into my app like you said you'd do way back in 2024

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

More than two years after promising it, Apple lets developers test Siri AI app integration

As of Tuesday, developers can explore expanded Siri AI integration for their Apple platform apps through beta releases of iOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, macOS 27.3, and visionOS 27.3. Next spring, nearly three years after announcing plans for deeper third-party Siri integration, Apple expects to begin accepting App Store submissions using the new capabilities.

"This developer beta offers a sneak peek at Siri AI app intents for developers," Apple said in a developer news post. "With this early Developer Preview, your app can work directly with Siri AI, giving you a first look at new ways to reach and serve your customers."

Anything is likely to be an improvement. Apple's Siri, a voice-activated assistant service, dates back to 2011 and gained limited third-party app access in 2016. In 2024, it became an interface for interacting with Apple Intelligence, its suite of generative AI features.

Apple Intelligence has not been very intelligent – several advertised Siri capabilities were delayed, so much so that Apple was sued in 2025 over allegations of misleading marketing. After a shakeup of Apple's machine learning group in December 2025, Siri and Apple Intelligence received a makeover at Apple's Worldwide Developers Conference in June.

"Before this year, Siri was a timer with a voice," said Roberto Gutierrez, Apple platforms staff engineer at a media company and author of the e-book On-Device Intelligence, in an email to The Register. "Weather, alarms, calls. Anything past that was a coin toss.

"On the developer side, SiriKit covered a handful of domains and Shortcuts only worked if users memorized the phrase. Siri AI is the first version that understands context and can chain actions across apps. That's a real change."

There is a catch, however, Gutierrez observed. Siri AI is available in a limited number of languages and regions. Support for Traditional Chinese is expected by the end of 2026, and EU availability remains a matter of negotiation – Siri AI is unavailable on iOS, iPadOS, and watchOS in the bloc as Apple works through interoperability concerns under Europe's Digital Markets Act.

"Apple is working constructively with the EU in an attempt to find a path forward," the company said last month without committing to any concessions.

Gutierrez said that he expects opening Siri to third-party developers will be useful to some extent.

"Siri can only do what apps expose through App Intents, and SiriKit is on its way out," he said. "If your app's main actions aren't exposed, the new Siri can't see you. The bigger shift is letting Claude, Gemini and others plug in. Developers already choose between three options for every AI feature: Apple's system model, an open model through MLX, or a cloud API. Siri is heading the same way. It doesn't need to be the smartest model. It needs to be the best router. That's the right call."

Apple has made choosing between those three options more difficult by failing to publish benchmarks that show how its Foundation Models compare with open-weight alternatives.

The biz has published measurements of how its third-generation Foundation Models compare to their 2025 predecessors. But the company's commitment – to "[share] more details on our latest foundation models, including updated evaluations and benchmarks, in a technical report later this summer" – remains unfulfilled so far as we're aware.

Gutierrez ran his own benchmarks to compare Apple's iOS system model (FM system, ~3B) to several open-weight models that are suitable for use on-device.

"On an A17 Pro iPhone running iOS 26, I tested Apple's system model against Qwen3 1.7B and 4B, Llama 3.2 3B and Gemma 3 4B on a structured-output task," he said. "Speed: Apple's model ran at about 149 tok/s, roughly 3x the fastest open model (Qwen3 1.7B, about 46)."

Quality, he said, was a tie, with every model passing five typed checks. But memory, he said, was where Apple's models had an advantage.

"The system model's weights live outside your app's memory budget," he explained. "My app peaked at 12 MB during inference. An open 4B model costs 2.4-2.7 GB, and a 2.4 GB model plus your app plus a WebView is a crash report. So yes, it's competitive, and on iPhone it's the default I'd recommend. If you want your own model, Qwen3 1.7B is the one."

But Gutierrez urged Apple to publish proper competitive benchmarks. "Their numbers compare against their own previous models," he said. "Developers aren't choosing between Apple 2025 and Apple 2026. We're choosing between Apple, Qwen and a cloud API. I had to build my own harness to answer a question Apple could settle with one table."

The absence of competitive model metrics is not the only gap. Apple has also failed to accommodate web apps.

"Web apps are locked out," said Gutierrez. "Foundation Models is a Swift API (and now Python). No JavaScript, no WebKit. A PWA's only option is to ship its own model into the browser and pay the full memory bill inside a WebView."

And bringing your own model may pose performance problems. "On an A17 Pro iPhone, the 3-4B open models pushed the phone into 'fair' thermal state and dropped to 12-24 tok/s," he said. "In practice, your own model on iPhone means ~2B or smaller."

With Siri AI unavailable on iPhones, iPads, and Apple Watches in the EU (and its rollout in mainland China subject to regulatory requirements), developers may need a fallback for global apps to handle regional restrictions. Apple's Private Cloud Compute offers eligible developers access to Foundation Models, Gutierrez observed, though it requires membership in the App Store Small Business Program, fewer than two million first-time App Store downloads across their apps, and the necessary entitlement. ®

All About Bonnie-Chance Roberts, Beanie Feldstein’s Wife

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Beanie Feldstein just shared a major life update: She and her wife, Bonnie-Chance Roberts, quietly welcomed their first child. “She is genuinely beyond my wildest dreams. I really did dream her,” Feldstein told People of their baby girl in November 2026.

In May, the actress announced her pregnancy on Instagram, writing, “Limited Edition Scouse Beanie Baby coming soon!! 🎀🍼.”

The announcement comes nearly two years after the couple married in a camp-themed celebration in upstate New York.

Feldstein often keeps the spotlight on her work, but when it comes to Roberts, she doesn’t hold back. Over the years, she’s shared glimpses into their relationship, from how they met on set to building a life together.

So who is Bonnie-Chance Roberts? Here’s everything to know about Feldstein’s wife and their life as parents.

Originally from Liverpool, Roberts works behind the scenes in the entertainment industry as a producer.

Her credits include projects like Mary Queen of Scots, Bridget Jones’s Baby, and How to Build a Girl—which she worked on as a producer while Feldstein starred in it.

Feldstein and Roberts first connected while working on How to Build a Girl. Feldstein told Vogue in June 2023 that they initially met over Skype during the casting process. “Bon had been developing Caitlin Moran’s book How to Build a Girl into a movie for over five years when they finally started the casting process for Caitlin’s fictional proxy, Johanna, a 16-year-old girl from Wolverhampton, England,” Feldstein said. “With a dash of luck and a push from the universe, they ended up being open to meeting a Jewish girl from Los Angeles.”

Their working relationship quickly shifted. As Feldstein recalled to Vogue, “From the moment we first kissed, we both knew we would get married and months after that, Bon had announced that when the time came, she wanted to be the one to propose to me.”

The relationship grew steadily from there. By 2020, Feldstein publicly celebrated Roberts on Instagram, writing in a birthday tribute, “I’m pretty convinced you’re actually made of magic. The girl with the warmest heart, the most expansive imagination, and the most exceptional brain. I love you so much. Happy birthday, bubba!!!”

The couple didn’t keep things private for long. In September 2019, they stepped out together at the Toronto International Film Festival for the premiere of How to Build a Girl, marking their first red carpet appearance as a couple.

Roberts later posted about the experience on Instagram, writing, “They say it takes a village to build a girl, and they are right!”

Roberts took the lead when it came time to propose. Feldstein announced their engagement in June 2022, sharing photos from the moment on Instagram and captioning them, “I do, Bon.”

Roberts later described designing the ring during a long period when the couple lived apart due to the pandemic. “The dream was for the ring to look as if a shard of magic was frozen in time,” she told Vogue in 2023.

The couple tied the knot on May 20, 2023, at Cedar Lakes Estate in New York’s Hudson Valley.

The theme felt personal. Feldstein told Vogue, “I grew up going to summer camp for 10 years, and my parents and both sets of my grandparents met at summer camp, so camp is a lineage of love through the generations of my family. Even though we met in London and fell in love on a film set, to get married at a camp was a truly beautiful emotional homecoming.”

Roberts wore a tailored bridal suit for the ceremony, while Feldstein opted for a vintage-inspired Gucci gown.

Looking back on the day, Feldstein shared on Late Night with Seth Meyers in February 2024, “[My wedding] went so swimmingly,” she said. “Like, I’m such an anxious girly. And I was more calm at the wedding than I am, like, brushing my teeth, at the supermarket. I was so calm. I just got to look at her and hear her vows, and it was perfect.”

“We wrote our own vows, and it actually rained a little the day of our wedding, and we handwrote the vows and the trees sort of shook and dripped on my vows,” she continued. “And I was like, ‘Oh, I hope I know them by heart.’ But it was fine. It was actually all going perfectly.”

In May 2026, Feldstein and Roberts announced they were expecting their first child.

The couple shared the news with a joint Instagram post featuring a series of photos, including one of a cake decorated with pink bows and the message “B+B are having a baby!”

The announcement marked a new chapter for the couple—and a years-long dream realized for Feldstein. In 2021, the actress told Bustle, “Sixteen years from now I hope to be living in New York City or London, married to my girlfriend, and have babies and puppies running around while we both make movies, shows, and plays that feel meaningful and exciting and profound.”

Feldstein quietly welcomed her and Roberts’s baby girl. In a November 4 interview with People, she opened up about parenthood for the first time. She did not share her child’s name or birth date but marveled about life with her.

“She is genuinely beyond my wildest dreams. I really did dream her,” Feldstein said. “I always wanted to be a mom, and I always wanted a daughter. I look at her every day, and I’m like, ‘You’re just beyond anything I could have even tried to imagine.’”

“I love getting to watch her take in the world; it’s just the greatest gift. And getting to be a mom alongside my wife is the greatest gift,” she added. “She’s such a good mom, and it’s so special to watch. Motherhood is unbelievably beautiful.”

She reflected on one milestone she saw a few weeks before her interview. “There’s nothing like the first smile,” she said. “I could feel her soul from the moment she was born. I remember people told me newborns are little blobs and they don’t really come alive until 6 weeks. I disagree with that completely. I could feel her soul from the moment I met her, but when you get to see that externalized in the smile, it’s so magic.”

Feldstein doesn’t shy away from expressing how much Roberts means to her.

Speaking to People in February 2024, she said of married life, “My favorite part of married life is everything, but I think the big thing that felt different was saying ‘my wife.’ [It] has more power than like ‘my girlfriend’…you know, it’s like ‘my wife’ is like ‘oh, this is legit.’”

She added, “Oh my god, I just want her with me every second of every day.”

In her November 2026 interview with People, she touched on her gratitude raising a child with Roberts. “I don’t think anyone that has had a baby can look at their child and not just feel the most gratitude to the universe,” Feldstein said. “I feel very grateful every day that I get to be her mom and mother alongside my wife and watch her with my whole family.”

Ford Fathom EV pickup prices will range from $28,350 to the high $30Ks

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ford’s upcoming Fathom electric pickup will be among the most affordable EVs in the US, starting at $28,350. Even pricier trims will start at under $40,000.

After announcing the name of its new midsize electric pickup this summer, Ford said the Fathom will start at just $28,350 for the standard-range model.

Even with the $1,595 delivery fee, prices will start at under $30,000. On the company’s third-quarter earnings call last week, CEO Jim Farley said the Fathom, Ford’s first vehicle off the Universal Electric Vehicle (UEV) platform, remains on track to launch in Q4 2027.

Last Tuesday, a day before earnings, Farley said he had the chance to test-drive a prototype and that it “completely blew me away.”

The 0 to 60 mph, huge room inside, and the hands-free, ADAS experience was like “something I’ve never experienced,” Farley told investors.

Ford’s CEO added that the Fathom is a “breakthrough product” that’s highly capable and affordable. The MSRP under $30,000 and low cost of ownership, Farley said, is perfect for today’s market.

The electric pickup will use a zonal electrical architecture and a 48-volt system to cut costs. It will also offer bidirectional charging, enabling it to power your home to save on energy costs or during an outage.

When asked how the company plans to upsell the Fathom beyond the $28,350 starting price, Andrew Frick, president of Ford Model e, offered some insight.

“The starting price of $28,350 is just that,” Frick said. “We’ll have a range that goes to the high $30,000 range. And we’ll have a couple of different offerings within that,” he explained.

Frick said it’s too soon to share more about the offerings themselves at this point, and more details will come closer to launch.

According to Farley, the embedded Apple Maps is a “game changer” for any EV, but especially for a vehicle starting under $30,000.

“That is going to be the most exciting upsell opportunity,” Farley said, “it’s really the long-term subscription and digital experience for the customer.”

Apple Maps will be directly integrated into the navigation system. All Fathom models will be BlueCruise-capable for hands-free driving with standard Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

We still haven’t seen the interior, but Ford claims the Fathom has a “large touchscreen display” with easy-to-use navigation.

I almost feel like the Fathom will be utilized similarly to how the Maverick is utilized and possibly become a big seller as well. It seems well suited for a small business work vehicle as well with fuel savings by going electric to add to the appeal.

The midsize electric pickup will offer more interior space than a Toyota RAV4, and that’s not including the frunk (front trunk) or truck bed. Ford also said the Fathom will cost less to own over five years than a three-year-old used Tesla Model Y.

We also know the Fathom will have an NACS port after a prototype was caught at a Tesla Supercharger a few months ago.

Ford is set to open preorders in early 2027, with an official launch expected in the fourth quarter of 2027.

All the Exciting Ballot Wins You May Have Missed From Election Day

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The 2026 midterms featured a number of key issues on states’ ballots

The midterm general elections held this Tuesday marked a major shift for the United States. A historic “blue wave” reflected the country’s overwhelming support for the Democratic Party, which now has control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate. And a number of Democratic candidates won their elections for governor too, in swing (and even non-swing) states like Texas, Georgia, Iowa, and Ohio.

Many of the surprise wins also brought about historic firsts for the nation. Keisha Lance Bottoms has become the first Black woman ever elected to governor, while Chris Pappas is the first openly gay candidate elected to the Senate. Other notable firsts include the first-ever Muslim Senate-elect (Abdul El-Sayed) and the first-ever Native American female governor to be voted in to the post (Deb Haaland).

But with so much excitement around the candidates, it can be easy to forget that yesterday also saw a number of important measures featured on ballots across all 50 states. And a few of the results were quite uplifting.

Virginia saw a complete victory for voters on the left, with citizens of the state voting to codify marriage equality, even in the case that the Supreme Court ever decides to overturn Obergefell v. Hodges; and Loving v. Virginia, which protects interracial marriage, was also codified. Virginia voters also chose to protect reproductive rights, and to restore voting rights to those who have been previously incarcerated.

Nevada citizens voted to protect abortion rights too, while those in Washington voted not to repeal a millionaire wealth tax. (The key issues that many of the ballots centered around this year included abortion rights, taxes, drug policies, voting, and transgender rights.)

There are still some ballot measures that have yet to be called, and votes will continue to roll in throughout the next few hours, meaning a few major issues remain hanging in the balance.

Be sure to revisit this page as more results come in, and check out all the historic firsts from the 2026 midterm general elections here.

Bob Dylan Dressed Like Timothée Chalamet to Meet the Pope

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Today, millions of Americans awoke to social media feeds awash with images of and commentary about a landmark event that seemed nigh-impossible a mere 24 hours earlier. I speak, of course, of this image:

As the nation began to digest the results of a historic election that seemed to herald the arrival of a new America, two of our very best Americans met for a private audience on the other side of the Atlantic: Bob Dylan, the Great American artist, and Pope Leo XIV, the first American Pope. Dylan is in the midst of yet another lengthy European tour—his strongest in some number of years, judging by recent bootleg tapes. On November 2, he played a show at Milan’s Unipol Dome, then jetted over to Rome ahead of a November 5 performance at the ​​Palazzo dello Sport. What better way to kill an off-day than a quick chat with the Vicar of Christ?

It wasn’t the first time Bob met a Pope, as some pointed out. In 1997, he performed for John Paul II at the World Eucharistic Congress in Bologna, donning a dapper Western suit and a fine Stetson for the occasion. He and JP2 made for a bit of an odd couple, the mysterious American gunslinger and the seemingly-nonplussed Polish holy man.

Dylan has somewhat more in common with Pope Leo, a.k.a Robert Francis Prevost, a fellow Midwesterner. But even if the two men hail from similar geography, the sight of them together under the bright fluorescent lights of an anonymous Vatican office looked like the product of a well-crafted AI prompt, if not an outright fever dream. The images were so unexpected, so uncanny, I would’ve been convinced they were fake had they not been published by an official press organ of the Vatican.

Much of the whiplash stemmed from the two men’s appearances. Angel and devil. Spy versus spy. The man in the long black coat and the man in the short white mozzetta. The one liners wrote themselves.

On Wednesday, the Pope wore a clean white cassock and a tidy little zucchetto, with an iron cross hanging down around his neck. In short, he looked like the Pope. I suppose I would say the same about Bob: that he looked like himself. As any good Dylan fan knows, however, the meaning of that phrase has changed radically over the years. Bob’s shifting appearance is almost as notorious as his shifting artistic sensibility. Dust bowl refugee, sharp-suited mod, bohemian poltergeist, whatever this is—the man has worn many hats.

A hoodied Timothée Chalamet, seen with Kylie Jenner, in New York City.

Sometime in the early 1990s, Dylan took a liking to the humble hoodie, a stylistic evolution that intersected neatly with his booze-fueled artistic nadir. The hoodie allowed him to hide in plain sight, even as he continued to take the stage around the world on his Never Ending Tour. It was practical, yes, but it was also a defense mechanism, a way to synthesize his compulsion to perform and his aversion to being seen. That it often looked hard as hell did not hurt. (It should come as little surprise that Timothée Chalamet, who may as well have been born wearing a hoodie, seemed to riff on this era when he was channeling Bob all those months ago.)

In the 21st century, Bob came unstuck from time. On landmark records like “Love And Theft” and Modern Times, Dylan burrowed into an imaginary musical past situated somewhere between the Civil War and the Great Depression. His style shifted to match. In the early aughts, he adopted a full country-western guise, complete with a Lee Van Cleef mustache. In the years that followed, his sartorial inspirations shifted east: a little less cowboy, a little more riverboat gambler, often performing in wide-brimmed hats, long frock coats, and string ties. “Rock and roll Mark Twain” wouldn’t have been an inaccurate descriptor.

Which brings us back to today’s meeting with Pope Leo. On first glance, Dylan’s ensemble is bonkers: western black blouson with contrast stitching along the chest and collar; beneath that, a heavyweight black hoodie zipped to the chest; beneath that, a white collared shirt with several buttons undone, collar loosely drawn together with a polka-dot cravat. The hood is decidedly up, stationed atop a black flat brim cap. In some shots, Bob can even be seen sporting leather gloves and an old-fashioned umbrella, which seems to double as a cane.

Part “Man in Black” sobriety, part rock star debauchery, with a dash of the hypebeast flavor from Timmy’s A Complete Unknown press tour—the man got a damn fit off in Vatican City. There’s no other way to describe it. (It’s a shame Pope Leo left his Rhude jacket back at the Papal Palace.)

Still, it’s a look with roots in Bob’s past, or pasts: the hoodies of the ’90s, the Westernwear of the 2000s, even the polka dots of 1965. No one but Bob would ever dare to pair all these disparate elements, particularly for a private audience with the Bishop of Rome. No one but Bob could.

Someone should come up with a word for that.

Salomon Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX Review: Built for Wet, Gritty Miles

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The waterproof Gore-Tex upper and grippy lugs handle sloppy gravel—and slick floors during long shifts.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

The Salomon Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX is technically a gravel running shoe, and it excels in that arena. It’s also becoming an absolute cult favorite among nurses, techs, and other healthcare workers who spend 12 hours on slick floors and want something wipeable, grippy, and weather/other substances-tough. Personally, I tested it on the one surface it was born for: wet, gritty, cursed early-morning gravel that always tries to murder me. Tough luck, gravel gods. I’m still alive.

The Gore-Tex of the Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX is the cold, wet, ugly-conditions sibling in Salomon’s trail running gravel lineup. It’s a boss of a gravel trainer that shares its midsole, outsole, drop, and stack with the standard Grvl running shoe; the difference is that Gore-Tex upper, which trades some airflow and a little weight for weather and moisture blocking power. If you train through damp autumns and slushy winters, like I do, the GTX is 100 percent what you want. If you run mostly in dry, warm conditions, save the $20 and some sweat and take the regular version.

When it comes to the Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX, the lugs make the shoe. At 2.5 mm, the lugs aren’t the aggressive, mud-clawing teeth you’ll get on a mountain shoe. They’re short, chevron-shaped, and made of Contagrip rubber that gives you enough hold without digging, and they’re short enough that road-to-trail is totally doable.

I ran them at Shirley Chisholm Park in Brooklyn, which is a landfill-turned-park full of gravel hills. The conditions were damp, slippery, and gloomy. I took them out in the early morning, with loose stone over wet packed base. It’s weather that makes you wonder why you’re there and not at the TJ Maxx across the highway. But, even as a Maxxinista, I settled in fast and happy because the lugs gripped like they were made of sticky tack. My uphills weren’t tripping me up and pulling my toes under my knees, and the downhills didn't turn into a gravel slide. They have just enough give and flex in that rock-protected sole to keep my wobbly body upright, in spite of what gravity would want me to do.

Short lugs also mean less trapped debris and more contact area, which is why the grip carries over to hard, wet surfaces. That’s a big part of the healthcare-worker love: it’s a grippy sole that doesn't feel like a clunky work clog or like you’re rounding in cleats.

The optiFoam² midsole sits in the moderate-cushion zone. It’s soft enough to take the edge off a long shift or a long run, and firm enough that it doesn't feel mushy on uneven stone. It’s not quite a Goldilocks for all situations, but where it works it sings. The 8mm drop is traditional and calf-friendly, so it suits people who are on their feet all day.

It’s not terribly responsive overall, but somehow, in spite of that, it feels fast on gravel, especially on the trickier uphills, where you lose a lot of oomph in most hybrid shoes. It has a nice “settle into your stride” ride feel that gives you a lot of pep for the price. It’s not a racer, but I could see myself racing in them.

The weather in the mornings was, for a period of time, frankly, unholy. My feet stayed dry and comfortable the entire time. My body: not so much. The Gore-Tex membrane handles puddle splash, wet grass, and gravel dust (read: concrete) without seepage, and the upper is easy to wipe down, which is the second reason it’s popular in hospitals and clinics: spills, splashes, and grime aren’t likely to be permanent.

The trade-off is the standard Gore-Tex trade-off: it’s just less dang breathable than a mesh upper, so it runs warmer. Fit-wise, it’s a standard width suitable for most runners—save for those with a really wide midfoot—with a secure, wraparound lockdown.

There’s a quirk to gravel nobody chats about, but I obsess over—the aforementioned concrete situation. The quickLace neo bungee system is great for quick, adjustable fit, but wet gravel dust can dry on the bungee and basically cement it in place. The laces get stuck.

The fix: wet them down a little. A bit of water de-cements the dust and the system moves freely again. Rinse your shoes after dusty runs and you’ll never notice.

Here’s where I must be honest about the limits. This is not the shoe for flat, slabby rock or highly technical trails. The lugs are too shallow to bite into loose, steep, or rooty terrain, and the sole isn’t quite flexible enough to conform around rock features. If your trails look like a scramble, look at a deeper-lugged trail shoe instead.

And it looks good doing it. The number of these I see on chic New Yorkers in my office is wild. But, the important bits, like the abrasion-resistant upper, hold up to gritty, scuffy use, and the reflective details are aces for dark early-morning starts.

Is the Salomon Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX waterproof? Yes. It uses a Gore-Tex membrane, so it’s waterproof and wind-resistant.

Is it a trail shoe or a road shoe? It’s a hybrid gravel shoe. It’s at home on gravel, fire roads, and mixed paths, but it isn’t designed for technical, rocky trails.

Are Salomon Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX shoes good for nurses? Many healthcare workers swear by the third version and are grabbing the fourth for the grip. It’s easy to clean and moisture-resistant.

GTX or standard Grvl? Pick the GTX for wet, cold conditions. Pick the standard version for dry, warm weather and better airflow.

The Aero Blaze 4 Grvl GTX is a stylish, abrasion-resistant, grippy gravel shoe that doubles as a great on-your-feet-all-day option. It’s not a technical trail mistress, or a trail racer, and it isn’t trying to be. For gritty gravel, fire roads, and messy mixed terrain in lousy weather, it’s one of the best new choices around.

The STJV union says Microsoft imposed hiring freezes at Arkane that resulted in delays and additional expenses, which it then cited as reasons for shutting it down.

"After freezing hiring for 19 months, which artificially caused delays and additional cost, Microsoft now uses those same delays and additional costs as a reason to close down the studio."

France's Syndicat des Travailleureuses du Jeu Vidéo (STJV) union has accused Microsoft of sabotaging work on Marvel's Blade and then using the inevitable results to justify Arkane's closure.

The union said development of Blade "is going fine," and that the game is on par with Arkane's previous body of work, which includes the Dishonored series, Prey, Deathloop, and the sadly overlooked Dark Messiah of Might and Magic. Despite that, "workers have to face the harsh reality: our studio might get closed because, and only because, Microsoft decided to, even if that means sabotaging the game it funded."

"Microsoft chose to justify the closure on economic reasons," the union wrote. "But Microsoft is actually why those economic reasons even exist. Because after freezing hiring for 19 months, which artificially caused delays and additional cost, Microsoft now uses those same delays and additional costs as a reason to close down the studio."

Closing Arkane would defy "financial logic," the union claimed, as it would likely cost more money than just allowing it to finish Blade—and furthermore, it added, Arkane is profitable—"just not profitable enough for Microsoft."

"Microsoft is responsible for the situation Arkane is in today. Microsoft is responsible for the situation the workers are in today," the union said. "So Microsoft has to take full responsibility here and act accordingly."

While its "dearest wish" is to see Arkane "live on," STJV said the studio's future has to go beyond just completing Blade. And if that's not feasible and Arkane must close, it falls to Microsoft—"the company who decided that Arkane has to shut down"—to take care of the hundreds of lives it has thrown into turmoil. Given Microsoft's essentially limitless resources, the union believes the company has a moral obligation to go beyond the bare minimum required by law: "We will not allow the lives of Arkane Studios' workers to be destroyed without the company paying a fair price."

The union is not aiming low with its demands. First, "to give employees control over the decisions that shape their future" while acquisition negotiations are underway:

Should Arkane ultimately be closed, it demands Microsoft "funds commensurate with Microsoft’s status as a corporation" to ensure employees are properly compensated:

Those are big asks, but the union isn't wrong about the resources Microsoft has at its disposal: Microsoft's net income for the first quarter of FY27 was $36.6 billion. Xbox is a small contributor to that total, but it is a part of Microsoft, and that makes it Microsoft's issue to deal with.

For now, the fate of Arkane remains unresolved: The studio fell victim to the most recent round of cuts at Xbox, but a recent report indicated that it might yet find a home with a new owner. I've reached out to Xbox for comment on the STJV union's demands and will update if I receive a reply.

Game Theory Helps SETI Search for Alien Civilizations

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Search for Extraterrestrial Intelligence (SETI) is mostly concerned with where to look for technosignatures from another civilization. Between 2012 and 2015, SETI's Project Phoenix targeted about 1,000 Sun-like stars for signs of Intelligent life. But where to look might not be as important as when to look, according to scientists at SETI.

In a new paper, scientists outline the effort to search for signals not only in the right place, but at the right time. The paper is "A Technosignature Survey Of The Anti-Solar Point: The SETI Institute's Largest-Ever Campaign With The Allen Telescope Array," and it was presented at the 77th International Astronautical Congress. The authors are Isabel Gerrard and Sofia Z. Sheikh. Gerrard is the Technosignature Researcher with Breakthrough Listen at the University of Oxford, and Sheikh is a SETI Institute Research Scientist.

âThe challenge in SETI isnât simply deciding where to point a telescope. Itâs figuring out where and when another civilization might choose to transmit." - Dr. Sofia Sheikh, SETI Institute Research Scientist.

The new search effort doesn't do away with the "where" aspect of the search. Since life exists around stars, this search is still focused on stars, or rather the planets that orbit them. But it's different this time. This time it involves a point changes over time, called the antisolar point. It's the point in space-time that's directly opposite from the Sun from the perspective of an observer.

Imagine a line extending from the Sun, through an observer on Earth, and out the other side. It's not fixed in place; it's an abstract point defined by changing coordinates that shifts as the Earth orbits the Sun.

This simple static illustration shows the antisolar point. (created with AI)

This new SETI effort is also inspired by game theory. Game theory is a way of anticipating another player's moves in a game, when their moves are also based on their anticipations of your next move. The most well-known example of game theory is probably the Prisoner's Dilemma. In this case, the other player is another intelligent civilization conducting their own SETI-like effort.

What it boils down to is trying to predict when and where we can detect a purposeful transmission from another civilization and send our own, and also when and where they're doing the same.

"We borrow from game theory the concept of Schelling points to hypothesize spacetime coordinates where both we and an extraterrestrial agent might send and search for a signal," the authors write. Schelling points are also called focal points in game theory. "From the perspective opposite the Sun, an extraterrestrial agent could detect Earth transiting within the habitable zone, thus this Anti-Solar Point (ASP) poses a shared and salient Schelling point."

Resources are limited, and the better we or an alien civilization can use them, the greater the chances of success. If they can figure out the most effective time and place to send a radio signal, they increase their chances of success. The same is true for us. There's a synergy in it.

"A Schelling point is a default solution two agents both naturally arrive at, without communication, because of some shared cultural understanding of prominence in the given context," the authors write. "The predictability of a Schelling point relies on a common understanding of the prominent features in this context, and the mutual awareness of each otherâs knowledge."

So without any prior communication, both we and another extraterrestrial agent would identify the same place to send and receive signals: the antisolar point. The choice is based on our shared understanding of astronomical transits, or what we call the transit method in the hunt for exoplanets. It's almost certain that another civilization would also use this method because it presents itself naturally.

"It follows that if an ETA (Extraterrestrial Agent) also used the transit method in their search for life, they would detect Earth against the Sun as habitable and classify Earth as an eligible target for interstellar communication," the researchers explain.

This simple animation shows how the antisolar point moves and how an exterrestrial observer would detect Earth's transit. (AI-generated)

At the midpoint of Earth's transit in front of the Sun, from the ETA's perspective, Earth would be directly between them and the Sun. "From the perspective opposite the Sun, once each year an extraterrestrial agent could detect Earth transiting within the habitable zone, thus this antisolar point poses a shared and salient time-coordinate Schelling point," the authors write.

The search using the Allen Telescope Array and the antisolar point is called the ATA-ASP survey. It's a multiyear effort, and its initial findings are already here. Because it's based on the ASP, the survey isn't constant, it's intermittent. As Earth orbits the Sun, the ASP keeps changing, and it takes several years to cover it.

One of the Allen Telescope Array's dishes. The ATA-ASP survey will be the ATA's largest survey effort, collecting 11 petabytes of data over four years. Image Credit: Photo taken by Isabel Gerrard and edited by Ananya Kalapatapu

"Provided that half of the ecliptic is inaccessible in a year (the portion that is daytime), the campaign requires at least two years of observing sessions to sample every possible ASP along the ecliptic," the authors explain. "Furthermore, due to the â¼ 365.25 day orbit of the Earth (inciting the leap day every 4 years), the accessible nighttime portion of the ecliptic slightly precesses in successive years, ergo total survey coverage requires staggering observations over the course of four years." For these reasons, the ATA isn't solely occupied with the ATA-ASP survey.

The first pilot results are from August August 30th, 2024 until August 25th, 2026. Obviously, ATA-ASP didn't detect any alien signals or we would've almost certainly heard about them already. But for SETI, the results mark a milestone. Searching the ASP for signals was first proposed decades ago, and now they've finally done it.

ATA-ASP detected an enormous number of radio signals, about 1.9 million in 17 hours of observations. But they were all from human sources. Those detections were still an important test of the system though.

As things stand now, the ATA-ASP campaign has gathered another 121 nights of observations since the initial pilot. Those results haven't been shared yet.

âThe challenge in SETI isnât simply deciding where to point a telescope,â said co-author Sheikh. âItâs figuring out where and when another civilization might choose to transmit. The anti-solar point gives us a way to think about that problem from the perspective of a potential sender, rather than just the receiver.â

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

The code for YouTube’s Android app reveals a testing ground for Instagram-style social features

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The most recent version of YouTube‘s Android app includes some curious lines of code. A report from Android Authority reveals numerous references to Tamba, a mysterious entity that has some similarities to Instagram.

Android Authority‘s discoveries within the YouTube app include the logo pictured above and dozens of icons that share a crude aesthetic reminiscent of hand-drawn images. Those assets are tied to a package titled “com.google.android.youtube.sandbox,” suggesting that Tamba is a separate experience from the primary version of YouTube.

Based on the use of the word “sandbox” in that string, one hypothesis is that Tamba is nothing more than an experimental platform used to test new features before they’re pushed to YouTube. But Android Authority notes that the code references a separate “test” package, which means that Tamba could be something more than a digital lab.

Daily industry updates, straight to your inbox.

Some of the terms used in the Tamba assets sound more like what you’d expect to see on Instagram rather than YouTube. There are references to followers, direct messages, and group chats. Tamba’s chats may also be able to integrate formats from the main version of YouTube, including Shorts.

If YouTube is trying to develop its own take on Instagram, it wouldn’t be the first tech company to utilize that strategy. Meta’s perceived abandonment of the qualities that made O.G. Instagram so popular have opened up opportunities for rivals. The photo app Dispo, for example, enjoyed a brief surge by telling its users that it’s not Instagram. More recently, TikTok embraced different types of photo content as part of an effort to revive the bygone Instagram experience.

YouTube, for its part, has built up its DMs as Instagram has turned those messages into a revenue stream. DMs haven’t traditionally been a big part of YouTube’s approach to community, but if Tamba is a real thing, it could put that form of correspondance front and center.

Of course, that’s a big if. YouTube runs all sorts of tests all the time, and it’s important to accept that Tamba could ultimately amount to little. But Instagram is eager to encroach on YouTube’s territory by allowing long-form video uploads. It wouldn’t be a surprise if YouTube returned the favor.

XRP crypto is currently trading at $1.28, up about +2% over the past 24 hours after a rocky period tied to the US Midterms and Democrats wiping the floor with the Republicans. However, as of right now, the market seems to be recovering and stabilizing.

Now add the thin order books of a market with no sellers, and parabolic moves stop being a metaphor.

Users can download the wallet, install it in their browser, and start engaging with the ecosystem directly. The network is live for testing, the explorer is fully operational, and the wallet enables easy connections, marking a major step toward a complete, integrated testnet experience.

After the Midterms, Millennial Optimism Makes Sense Again

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

None of us are the same people we were in 2008. But it's okay, just for a day, to bask in hope.

I have told this story many times in writing, but in 2008 I was so hopeful on election night that I stripped off my clothes with a bar full of people right before they called Pennsylvania for Obama, to take a picture and commemorate the moment. They took the photo; we got up off the sticky bar floor, pulled our clothes back on, and literally danced in the streets with the cops while the Obama reggaeton song played. This was peak millennial political optimism, and the rush of it could make you believe that all the contradictions of this country didn’t matter. That we were all moving forward together.

In 2008, I was a junior researcher at a Black history museum, a place where people thought deeply about legacy and historical sea change. I remember being so deeply excited, after seven years of political insanity post-9/11, that maybe the United States was finally waking up. It was a shock, then, to talk to my older coworkers, most of whom had been involved with or studied the Black radical tradition, and hear their cautious indifference to what felt like a coming revolution. “We’ll see,” was what most of them said—a pragmatism about this country that I didn’t fully understand back then. Back then I hoped, deep down, that what those older coworkers were experiencing was merely sour grapes.

In 2026, of course, we are all living through the political backlash that they were sure was coming, and the missteps of moderation during the Obama years that set the stage for Trump. I don’t really hope about politics anymore. Yes, I know, hope is a discipline. Hope is something that, by definition, you have to have when things are dire. But the way hope lives in my body—a frantic, desperate thing that plummets when it meets catastrophe—means national politics aren’t a good place for that emotion. When I talk about politics now with friends, it's mostly to see who can make the darkest joke about our current timeline.

But after last night, things are different. Like a lot of people, I’m allowing myself one calendar day to actually feel excited about the state of the world. I’m still saying “let’s see,” but for right now, every time someone tries to bring up why we’re still doomed, I keep scrolling. I can do bad all by myself tomorrow.

Since Trump entered the scene 10 years ago, there’s been an active disdain for sincerity in politics. The reasons for this are many. Trump and MAGA ran on the belief that becoming great could only be measured by how badly the people you hated were suffering. Those who would oppose Trump grew cynical about the national Democratic Party’s dance of fearmongering about the state of the country and steadfastly refusing to do anything meaningful about it when given the power to do so. Between these two extremes, it felt foolish to care. It felt smarter and safer to view politics the way a consultant or a pundit might, with the distance of someone who keeps telling you he has no skin in the game. As if that position was a superior one. As if that distance could save you any heartbreak, keep the threat of a layoff or a medical bill or an eviction at bay.

To care is to be cringe, and in the last few years, being cringe has been one of the greatest cultural sins, a devastating social faux pas. But last night was such a seismic shift in American politics that the idea of cringe is currently, blissfully, irrelevant. Being cringe enough to care brought millions to the polls and a historic midterm turnout. Being cringe enough to care meant that people like James Talarico, Abdul El-Sayed, and Angie Nixon launched campaigns even as everyone told their potential base that voting was irrelevant, that nothing could ever change about their states.

I’m not the same person I was in 2008—hopefully none of us are. And this is not the same country. But to make sure this country is one where I want to be present, I have to believe on some level that it’s possible. To change anything in this world, you first have to care enough to want to do it.

Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Coinbase Says 99% of Customer Crypto Is Offline and Out of Hackers’ Reach

Coinbase is moving to reassure customers that what happened at Binance can’t easily happen there.

In a blog post Wednesday, the U.S. exchange said it wasn’t affected by last week’s hack of Binance, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from the rival exchange’s hot wallets.

Coinbase said it keeps more than 99% of customer assets in cold storage, meaning offline wallets that can’t be reached over the internet. It moves funds into internet-connected hot wallets only as customers need them.

The Binance incident “has understandably left customers asking” how Coinbase guards against a similar attack, the post said.

Coinbase laid out several layers of protection. Private keys for its cold wallets are split into pieces and never fully reassembled, even when signing transactions, and no single employee has standing access to the hot-wallet signing service. Strict limits cap how fast funds can move from cold to hot wallets, and unusual withdrawal spikes are held until staff review them. The exchange can also trigger “kill switches” that take signing keys offline entirely.

The company said it assumes employees will be targeted by phishing, so its controls are designed so that a stolen password, session or device can’t be used on its own, and sensitive actions require multiple approvals.

That focus on insiders carries weight for Coinbase. In May 2025, it disclosed that criminals had bribed overseas customer support agents to steal personal data on less than 1% of its monthly transacting users, then demanded a $20 million ransom that Coinbase refused to pay.

Last week, Coinbase said a security incident at analytics vendor Amplitude exposed contact data for a “small subset” of customers, though funds weren’t affected.

Coinbase also said customer assets are held 1:1 and aren’t lent out unless customers direct it to, and it reported $8.6 billion in cash and cash equivalents and $377 billion in assets on its platform as of Sept. 30. It flagged artificial intelligence as a fast-changing threat and pledged further security investment.

The Binance theft, which the FBI has attributed to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, has rattled the industry. Blockchain analytics firm Elliptic found that the laundering spanned about 12,000 wallets in under four days, while stablecoin issuers froze about 44% of the haul.

Separately, Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin halted withdrawals after a $40 million hack, and French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa recently confirmed a cyberattack that led to a $133 million crypto theft.

Alcohol Isn’t the Only Way to Bond at Work. Try These 3 Healthy Alternatives Instead.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Here are three ways to build better bonds with each other that don’t rely on a tequila shot or a glass of chardonnay.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

“Shots, shots, shots! Come on, you can do a shot,” my former boss said, shouting at me at the makeshift bar. It was our quarterly offsite, and my boss wouldn’t take no for an answer. He had begun pressuring me and mocking me in front of the rest of the team. I felt like I was at a college frat party. I finally relented. I did the tequila shot. And for the remainder of my time working for this boss, I walked around team outings and happy hours and off-sites with a “vodka tonic.” I kept pretending to drink with a glass full of ice, filled with club soda and lime wedges, just so he would leave me alone.

And I am not the only one who feels this way. Apparently, 33% of employees don’t want to drink at work events. And like me, 15% of employees felt obligated to drink, so they grabbed a glass of wine, a beer or maybe even a shot. Being pressured to drink may create negative social energy, because not everyone drinks. Not everyone feels comfortable drinking with their boss. Employees may decide to disengage or skip out on happy hours and team bonding events centered around alcohol altogether.

Alcohol shouldn’t be the only way we form connections at work. Start with these three ways to build better bonds with each other at work, that doesn’t include a shot of tequila or a glass of chardonnay.

According to research from Wellhub, a corporate wellness platform, “wellness hour” is the new happy hour. In fact, Tuesdays at 6 p.m. is the peak time for wellness across the globe, replacing the happy hour. Employees are more and more focused on nutrition, healthy habits, mental well-being and fitness.

“For a long time, team bonding meant happy hour,” says Cesar Carvalho, CEO and co-founder of Wellhub.  “I think that’s changing, and it should. Movement and recovery aren’t a break from the workday anymore. They’re what makes the rest of the day actually work.”

Instead of bonding over a glass of wine, do a spin class together, go for a run or try a yoga class. If you are traveling as a team and in a new city, consider a walking tour together to explore new neighborhoods. You can also make walking meetings part of your team’s traditions and step away from the keyboard. You can bond over physical activity rather than hanging out at an overcrowded bar.

I was pleasantly surprised to discover that a number of companies have started business book clubs, and my recent book was included: The Devil Emails at Midnight: What Good Leaders Can Learn From Bad Bosses. These were self-organized efforts, where employees reached out to let me know they were reading my book as a team, sending pictures and, in some cases, a summary of what they discussed.

According to Eventbrite, book club events have grown 31% from recent data from the platform. Book clubs are transforming social scenes, helping build friendships and connections in a time when people are seeking more community. Book clubs can help teach us new skills and also transform our connections at work too.

“Leaders continue to struggle with how to keep employees engaged and how to help employees learn and stay curious,” says Tracy Avin, Founder, TroopHR, a premier community for modern HR leaders to grow together. “Reading books together gives people something meaningful to bond over. Instead of asking, ‘What are we drinking?’ we’re asking, ‘What are we thinking about?’ That shift can create deeper conversations, stronger connections and a culture where people keep learning from one another long after the book club gathering is over.”

Volunteer together. In the past, I have organized volunteer days with Dress for Success, Habitat for Humanity, Big Brothers Big Sisters of America and Meals on Wheels America. You can also research and contact a food pantry, community garden or an animal shelter to build deeper connections locally. If you build a relationship with an organization, this doesn’t have to be a one-off. You can show up as a team once every quarter to make an impact and create stronger team connections.

I find that when I volunteer with my team, we are working toward something bigger than OKRs or annual goals. We spend time together, learn a new skill and watch how we can collectively make an impact. We also discover new things about each other. This can be far more meaningful than being pressured into doing tequila shots together.

We don’t have to drink together to bond at work. Some of us enjoy a glass of chardonnay, while others would prefer to just stick to a club soda. We need reasons to talk, laugh, celebrate and spend time together that don’t depend on what’s in our glasses. The best workplace cultures I have been part of don’t rely on the happy hour. They are built around how we meaningfully connect.

“Shots, shots, shots! Come on, you can do a shot,” my former boss said, shouting at me at the makeshift bar. It was our quarterly offsite, and my boss wouldn’t take no for an answer. He had begun pressuring me and mocking me in front of the rest of the team. I felt like I was at a college frat party. I finally relented. I did the tequila shot. And for the remainder of my time working for this boss, I walked around team outings and happy hours and off-sites with a “vodka tonic.” I kept pretending to drink with a glass full of ice, filled with club soda and lime wedges, just so he would leave me alone.

And I am not the only one who feels this way. Apparently, 33% of employees don’t want to drink at work events. And like me, 15% of employees felt obligated to drink, so they grabbed a glass of wine, a beer or maybe even a shot. Being pressured to drink may create negative social energy, because not everyone drinks. Not everyone feels comfortable drinking with their boss. Employees may decide to disengage or skip out on happy hours and team bonding events centered around alcohol altogether.

Alcohol shouldn’t be the only way we form connections at work. Start with these three ways to build better bonds with each other at work, that doesn’t include a shot of tequila or a glass of chardonnay.

As it expands, Provincetown’s Fine Arts Work Center prioritises access and affordability

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Fine Arts Work Center visual arts fellow Carlie Trosclair at work in a Provincetown studio Photo: Daisy St. Sauveur

Provincetown, the harbour community perched on the tip of the Cape Cod peninsula in Massachusetts, has a well-earned reputation as a haven for artists, but that status is under threat due to a lack of affordable housing. Geographically secluded and aesthetically distinctive, the town, which experienced an influx of Portuguese sailors in the 1850s, has attracted artists for almost a century, particularly queer painters, writers and poets.

Provincetown’s Fine Arts Work Center(FAWC) has been at the heart of this creative community since it was founded in 1968 by a group that included the painters Robert Motherwell, Myron Stout, Jack Tworkov and Fritz Bultman. The painter Sam Messer and Pulitzer Prize-winning poet Mary Oliver were among the centre’s early residents, followed in ensuing decades by artists including Lisa Yuskavage, Jack Pierson, Ellen Gallagher, Jeffrey Gibson, Firelei Báez, Elliot Hundley, Jacolby Satterwhite and Jennifer Packer.

Miguel Braceli, True love Kiss: a Queer Fairy Tale in Provincetown, 2024 Courtesy the artist

Today, the non-profit grants artists access and community in a town that is among the most expensive in New England during the summer season. Providing room and support for queer artists remains a priority. The Venezuelan artist Miguel Braceli, for instance, completed his 2024 residency by orchestrating the public performance True Love’s Kiss, in which strangers were invited to lock lips at the granite breakwater on the shore’s very western tip.

Located in the Provincetown’s residential east end, the FAWC is best known for its residency programme, which provides ten artist fellows and ten writers with lodging and studio space for seven months every year, between October and April. Among this year’s artist residents are Abigail Dudley, Alina Perez, Nasim Hantehzadeh and L.J. Roberts.

The Fine Arts Works Center's 1979-80 fellows in the studios Courtesy the Fine Arts Works Center, Provincetown, Massachusetts

“People have come here for generations to live their identities to the fullest and they have heavily contributed to the artistic creativity that makes Provincetown what is,” Sharon Polli , the centre’s executive director, tells The Art Newspaper. “The unique experience residents feel the second they drive down Route 6 or step off the ferry comes out in the work created here.”

The centre has been in a continuous state of expansion and renovation for much of its 58-year history. What was once the longtime Provincetown resident Hans Hoffman’s studio is now a fraction of the centre’s main grounds, named Days Lumberyard, which includes multiple living units, studios, offices, an auditorium, a gallery space, an outdoor area and a printshop that still houses Motherwell’s own press. The centre acquired a heating supply building in 1972 for around $120,000 and has gradually transformed its coal bins and other disused or dilapidated areas into its main hub.

The Days Lumberyard building at the Fine Arts Work Center in Provincetown, Massachusetts, date unknown Courtesy Fine Arts Work Center, Provincetown, Massachusetts

Polli says the lumberyard’s growth and importance to the community are proof of “Provincetown being a place where there has always been room for artists and writers who maintain the town’s cultural identity” since the middle of the 20th century. The centre operates on an annual budget of around $3m, $600,000 of which this year was raised at its annual summer gala at the Pilgrim Monument at the Provincetown Museum.

The centre’s core role in maintaining the town’s artistic and intellectual identity has recently been officially acknowledged. The municipal board made a pledge this past summer to grant the centre $1.5m toward a planned $12m transformation of its 4 Brewster Street building. Located around the corner from the main FAWC complex, the lot the centre acquired in 1997 is in line for a totally new build. The Boston-based firm Hacin Architects was selected to double the housing square footage for fellows and staff, while adding a community space and a rooftop garden.

The main Fine Arts Work Center complex in Provincetown, Massachusetts Photo: Robert Benson

“We are hoping that the municipal support will help us leverage private funding,” Polli says. She and her team of 12 full-time employees keep the conversation and cultural exchange active throughout the off-seasons by offering a community-oriented programming. The year-round calendar includes FAWC Fridays, group exhibitions with past fellows, Queer Week, workshops and public talks with artists such as Catherine Opie, Ilana Savdie and Doron Langberg.

Polli says the centre’s off-season programming is even “more important than anything we do rest of the year because we give people a place to engage with high-calibre dialogues where many locals have working practices”.

American Bitcoin (ABTC) Posts $126 Million Profit as Holdings Gain $197 Million

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

American Bitcoin Corp. (Nasdaq: ABTC) reported about $126 million in third-quarter net income on Wednesday, a swing from a $57.2 million loss in the prior quarter that rested largely on a $197.3 million gain on the company’s bitcoin holdings rather than on mining revenue.

The Miami-based miner and treasury company, a majority-owned subsidiary of Hut 8 Corp. and co-founded with Trump family backing, mined about 891 bitcoin in the quarter at a cost of roughly $42,100 each and ended September with about 8,427 BTC.

Mining revenue was about $63.4 million, down from about $67.0 million in the second quarter. Gross profit was about $25.8 million, a 41% margin, compared with about $33.0 million and 49% a quarter earlier, as seasonal power costs rose across its sites.

Adjusted EBITDA came in at about $221 million, compared with an Adjusted EBITDA loss of about $45.0 million in the second quarter, according to the company’s release.

Eric Trump, co-founder and chief strategy officer of American Bitcoin, said the quarter delivered the net income and Adjusted EBITDA figures while growing the bitcoin reserve and bitcoin per share. Chief Executive Mike Ho said the company kept mining at a structural discount to spot and raised satoshis per share by about 5% without issuing shares through its at-the-market program or buying bitcoin on the open market.

The income statement makes the driver plain. American Bitcoin booked a $197.3 million gain on digital assets in the third quarter, after a $71.2 million loss on that line in the second. Operating income was about $191.8 million. Other expense included a $64.3 million loss on derivatives.

Strip the mark-to-market move out of the picture and the operating story looks quieter. Revenue per bitcoin mined was about $71,100, down slightly from about $71,900 in the second quarter, and the cost to mine rose to about $42,100 from about $36,500, the company said.

Production of about 891 bitcoin was below the second quarter’s roughly 932 coins, which the company had called its highest quarterly haul on record when it reported a $57.2 million second-quarter loss alongside a climb in holdings to about 8,002 BTC.

American Bitcoin said it sold a portion of production to cover operating costs while still adding to its strategic reserve. Holdings rose by about 425 bitcoin, or about 5%, to roughly 8,427 as of Sept. 30. That total includes about 3,090 bitcoin pledged for miner purchases under agreements with Bitmain.

Management’s preferred yardstick is satoshis per share, which rose about 5% to roughly 11,572 from about 10,989 at the end of June. The share count reflects a 1-for-15 reverse stock split effected earlier this year on July 2.

The owned fleet stood at about 89,242 bitcoin miners with roughly 28.1 EH/s of capacity at quarter-end. American Bitcoin remains majority-owned by Hut 8, which on the same day reported its own third-quarter results and confirmed bitcoin sales used to fund share buybacks while shifting mining into the American Bitcoin vehicle.

Eric Trump and Donald Trump Jr. helped launch the venture with Hut 8 in 2025; earlier Bitcoin Magazine coverage has put the Trump family’s stake near 20%, with Hut 8 holding the rest. The company has climbed the corporate treasury ranks as it stacked coins after its Nasdaq debut.

Whether another quarter of bitcoin price gains can keep paper profits ahead of higher power costs and thinner mining margins is the question the release leaves open.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is above $80,500 and shares of American Bitcoin were down over 5.5% today.

So You’ve Been Invited to Documenta 16. Here’s Our Advice.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

As two documenta 15 artists who were mercilessly vilified due to our support of Palestine, here’s what you need to consider when you make your choice.

Over the past few months, several artist friends who have been invited to participate in the upcoming documenta 16 have contacted us for advice, asking whether they should accept, refuse early on, or accept for now and withdraw at a later stage. As two artists who participated in documenta 15, we understand the dilemma. An invitation to documenta is not something one receives thoughtlessly. It is hard to turn down, and these days, it is also becoming hard to accept.

A few years ago, exhibiting at documenta was considered a lifetime achievement. But after the multi-faceted attacks on Palestinian artists and works of pro-Palestinian art at documenta 15 — and the collapse of the German state’s moral standing through its support for the genocide in Palestine and considering Israel its Staatsräson (“reason of state”) — this assumption has been shattered. There is no meaningful distance between the German state and its art institutions: Almost every museum, Kunsthalle, and festival, documenta included, is funded by the federal, regional, or municipal government, and thus steered by the conditions those funders set. As such, artists have to seriously ask themselves whether participating in documenta 16 will negatively affect one's career and well-being.

Our friends ask us for advice because of what the two of us, among many others, went through during documenta 15. We were subjected to severe and vicious attacks by the German media, political activists, politicians, and the establishment, built on unfounded, fabricated, and destructive accusations of antisemitism, which eventually led to attacks and vandalism of our exhibition venues, alongside those of other artists. This experience left scars on our lives and careers, making it hard to apply for jobs, funding, or exhibition opportunities. The stress and burnout we had to endure as a result of these attacks were enormous, and the energy it took came from our work and our lives — that toll can’t be overstated. This is why we decided to stay anonymous in this piece: to avoid any return to the stress and fear we had to deal with, which still haunts us even after nearly five years.

To those friends who are asking, and others who are wondering, we will not tell you whether to participate in documenta 16 or not. This is your own decision about what you wish to lend your voice to — personally, politically, creatively. But we can tell you what we wish we had known, and what you need to consider when you make your choice.

Since the beginning of the current genocide in Palestine, the German cultural scene has been in crisis. It has lost its credibility and shown how much it functions as the soft hand of state power. It has no independence from state politics and is unable to stand up against the position of the state due to the way arts and culture are funded in Germany. In 2019, the Bundestag passed a non-binding resolution calling for federal funding to be withheld from any organization or project that supports Boycott, Divestment, Sanctions (BDS). Non-binding as it was, it became a political tool to suppress any institutional support for the Palestinian cause. Another resolution in November 2024 tied public funding to the International Holocaust Remembrance Alliance’s controversial definition of antisemitism — which documenta officially adopted in early 2025. The rest of Germany’s major cultural institutions, except for a few independent ones, bent with little resistance. Like the others, documenta depends on state and federal funding; and like the others, it has no spine. German institutions are struggling to emerge from this crisis and regain their credibility without revising their positions or standing up to political pressure. At this point and under these conditions, documenta as an institution will likely benefit more from your participation than you will.

A rare second exit: AI startup Suplari acquired by Docusign nearly 3 years after Microsoft spinout

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 4, 2026 at 12:43 pmNovember 4, 2026 at 12:44 pm

Some startups dream of getting acquired by a big tech company. Suplari has now done it twice.

Docusign on Tuesday announced its acquisition of the AI-powered spending analysis company, five years after Microsoft bought the Seattle-based startup and nearly three years after the Redmond tech giant spun the company back out to its founders.

Financial terms of the latest deal weren’t disclosed. Docusign said it plans to fold Suplari’s technology into its contract management platform, pairing the startup’s analysis of corporate spending and supplier data with Docusign’s Iris AI engine.

The idea is to find savings, track what suppliers agreed to deliver, and flag purchases that fall outside contract terms.

“Joining Docusign allows us to connect that intelligence more closely to the agreements that shape a company’s spending,” Suplari CEO Jeff Gerber said in a statement.

It’s a remarkable double-dip in startup M&A for a company that was applying AI to corporate spending years before the current agentic boom.

Suplari was co-founded in 2016 by Nikesh “Niki” Parekh, a Seattle tech veteran who previously held leadership roles at Trulia and Market Leader, along with Gerber, a longtime engineering leader, and product leader Brian White. Gerber and White had worked together at iConclude, an earlier startup that Gerber co-founded.

The startup, in its first incarnation, pulled together procurement and spending data scattered across corporate systems such as SAP, Workday and Oracle, and used machine learning to flag savings opportunities, supplier risks and other inefficiencies. The pitch was an alternative to having teams of analysts comb through the data in Excel or Tableau.

Gerber has traced the idea to his time at Apptio, the IT cost management company, where he helped lead machine learning work. That opened his eyes to the ways that connecting financial data with IT activity could turn up problems such as auto-renewing contracts and services still running for employees who had left.

By 2021, Suplari had raised about $18 million from investors including Madrona, Amplify Partners, Shasta Ventures, Two Sigma Ventures and Workday Ventures.

Microsoft acquired Suplari in July 2021, saying it would pair the technology with its Dynamics 365 business software. The startup had about 40 employees at the time.

Then ChatGPT arrived in November 2022. Microsoft redirected all of its new-product investment to Copilot, Parekh recalled in a blog post earlier this year.

Succeeding inside Microsoft would have meant rebuilding Suplari from scratch to deploy across 400 to 500 data centers, “exactly the kind of investment Microsoft was now reserving for Copilot,” he wrote. By then, Suplari had become core to the finance and procurement operations of 40 to 50 of Microsoft’s most strategic customers.

“Microsoft concluded, and we agreed, that customers would be better served if Suplari operated as an independent company again,” Parekh wrote.

Suplari’s founding team bought the company back in late 2023, with Microsoft retaining a minority stake. Gerber, Suplari’s original CTO, led the spinout and became CEO. He had been chief AI strategy officer for Microsoft’s Dynamics finance and operations products after the acquisition.

Parekh stayed at Microsoft, working on Copilot Studio and the Power Platform, before leaving in June 2025 to start the AI recruiting startup Provn.

Since the spinout, Suplari has added an AI agent that helps procurement teams act on what its software finds, Gerber said on an Art of Procurement podcast in June. Its customers include BT Group, Nordstrom and MediaNews Group, according to Docusign. The company has about two dozen employees, according to LinkedIn.

Gerber declined an interview with GeekWire after the acquisition announcement, citing a quiet period. In a statement, Docusign CEO Allan Thygesen said Suplari’s “technology and talent” will help Docusign connect what companies’ supplier agreements say with their actual spending.

Parekh has remained a Suplari shareholder and advisor. Asked about the deal, he said via email that generative AI is “enabling a renaissance in many ‘traditional’ systems of record” like enterprise resource planning and contract lifecycle management.

Connecting contracts with their outcomes, he said, “will be the future.”

As for Suplari’s own outcome, Parekh said he’s “ecstatic” about the startup’s second exit.

Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

Cat Wu to Lead Product Management for OpenAI’s Core Products

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Cat Wu announced on November 5, 2026 that she is joining OpenAI to lead product management for Core Products.

Wu said she respected OpenAI for showing that scaling works, bringing AI to more than a billion people and treating great products as central to its mission. She named Thibault Sottiaux, OpenAI’s product-management team and employees across the company as people she was excited to work with.

Her stated objective is to build agents that support users and their teams, keep users in control and give them more time for the people and work they love. She also invited continued feedback.

The move follows Wu’s November 3, 2026 announcement that she had left Anthropic. She joined the Claude Code effort two years earlier, when it was a small internal tool, after asking it to build a reinforcement-learning environment and steering it for tens of hours without manually writing code. In her account, usage developed from manual approval of permission prompts and Claude use focused on answering questions and writing tests toward default auto-approval, large migrations and building new products. She credited the Claude Code, Cowork and Tag teams, along with users whose feedback shaped what they shipped.

Sottiaux is named as OpenAI’s Core Products Lead in the June 11, 2026 announcement of OpenAI’s planned Ona acquisition, which would bring Ona’s secure cloud execution and orchestration technology into Codex. OpenAI reported at the time that more than 5 million people used Codex each week, up 400% from earlier in 2026, and that the tool had expanded beyond software development into research, analysis, building and automation. The announcement connected its next phase to work extending over hours or days rather than minutes.

The planned Ona integration would provide persistent environments where agents can access tools, systems and context over time, including work continuing inside a customer’s cloud environment when laptops are closed. OpenAI’s design is intended to let users check progress, provide direction, make decisions and review results remotely rather than remaining tied to the device or session where a task began.

For organizational deployments, the documented requirements are control over where agents run, what they can access, how credentials are scoped, how activity is logged and how work moves through review. Ona’s customer-controlled execution model would let agents operate inside an organization’s own cloud environment while OpenAI provides intelligence and orchestration. Those controls give Wu’s emphasis on user control a concrete enterprise context: delegation depends on the agent’s capabilities and on the boundaries around its work.

Greg Brockman, identified as OpenAI’s president and co-founder in OpenAI’s October 21, 2026 Google Cloud announcement, welcomed Wu after her post. Sottiaux also welcomed her and said he looked forward to their work together.

Wu’s career move connects complementary approaches to the same product problem. Her Claude Code account describes a progression from approving individual actions to delegating large tasks, while OpenAI’s documented agent plans combine persistent work with remote review and organizational controls. Her appointment brings that experience to Core Products, with the product challenge being how to expand delegated work while preserving users’ ability to direct, inspect and govern it.

Faux Fur Jackets Make a Chic Return and H&amp;M’s Chocolate Brown Style Elevates Any Outfit, From Jeans to Dresses

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Winter glamour, without the luxe price tag.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Faux fur has a habit of resurfacing every winter—because nothing else adds instant glamour to a casual outfit quite as quickly. This season the look has gone shorter and chicer, trading floor-sweeping drama for cropped, collared shapes you can bundle up in for a chilly commute and wear straight through to cocktails come evening.

H&M has the version we want. The Faux Fur Jacket sits at the waist and has a chic crop, so nothing interrupts all that plush texture. A funnel collar frames the face, there’s a lining inside for comfort, and it is perfect for tapping into the current chocolate brown trend.

Soft comes up in almost every review, closely followed by warm and stylish. Reviewers also said it is a bit cropped, so take that into account when picking sizing and how you want it to fit. A few also purchased the coat in multiple colors, which shows just how quality it is.

It fits true to size overall, though a few found it to run a little small on the arm length, so if you’re a taller gal you might want to size up as a precaution. The weight is light enough for milder winter days, with enough space for a chunky knit underneath when the temperature drops.

Chocolate brown is the one we’d buy first. That rich, chocolaty shade warms up denim, looks expensive, and complements black and cream alike. Navy blue is the party-season pick, and a fun addition if you’re looking for another fall color outside of the very popular chocolate brown.

Style the brown tonally, with a camel polo neck and tan boots, for that old-money winter vibe. Navy blue comes alive against black—think a velvet mini skirt, sheer tights, and pointed court shoes for the Christmas party.

By day, throw it over a fine-knit sweater, straight-leg jeans, and slouchy suede boots. For drinks or a date night, layer it over a slip dress with strappy heels and gold hoops. This is the perfect coat to carry with you throughout the fall and winter season when you want to elevate a simple outfit.
